1. Introduction 
General 
Bengaluru, one of India’s major metropolitan cities, has witnessed rapid urbanization driven by 
population growth, economic expansion, and spatial development. This has resulted in a steep rise in 
urban travel demand. Unfortunately, the surge in travel demand has led to several undesirable 
consequences, the most significant being the exponential growth in motor vehicle ownership. 
Simultaneously, the share of public transport has declined, with commuters increasingly relying on 
personal vehicles. This trend has imposed serious costs in the form of traffic congestion, travel delays, 
productivity losses, deteriorating air quality, and rising road accidents. 
Projections indicate that by 2031, Bengaluru’s population will far exceed current expectations, 
translating into nearly double the present volume of daily vehicle trips. This escalation poses a critical 
challenge to the city’s quality of life. Importantly, travel demand is closely linked to economic growth, 
which in Bengaluru is progressing at an even faster pace than population growth. Proactive measures 
are therefore essential to address the emerging situation. 
Over the past decade, Bengaluru has seen considerable investment in transport infrastructure, including 
IT parks, ring roads, flyovers, bridges, and underpasses. While these projects were intended to ease 
congestion, the city continues to face severe traffic bottlenecks. Vehicle registrations are increasing by 
400,000–600,000 annually, while the total road length remains largely unchanged. Several junctions 
have already exceeded their design capacity, necessitating immediate interventions to improve traffic 
flow. 
Urban transport in Bengaluru is predominantly road-based. National rail lines were not designed to serve 
urban and regional traffic, with infrequent stations and low service frequency. Road traffic is dominated 
by motorized two-wheelers and auto-rickshaws. Public transport, primarily operated by the Bangalore 
Metropolitan Transport Corporation (BMTC), functions in mixed traffic without dedicated lanes or 
signal priority. Many companies also operate private minibus fleets for employee transport. The BMTC 
network, though extensive, is diffuse and characterized by low service frequency. Metro connectivity 
through BMRCL is expanding, but remains insufficient to meet the city’s growing demand. 
Despite the construction of multiple flyovers and grade separators by the Bangalore Development 
Authority (BDA) and Bruhat Bengaluru Mahanagara Palike (BBMP), congestion persists. The city’s 
road network, largely laid out in the 1940s when the population was under half a million, is 
underdeveloped in terms of size, continuity, and connectivity. Today, links and intersections operate at 
or above capacity. Average journey speeds on key central roads during peak hours range between 5–
13.5 kmph, making daily commuting highly inefficient. Nearly all major junctions are over capacity, 
and traffic growth has far outpaced infrastructure expansion. 

=== PAGE 10 ===
 
4 
 
Bengaluru, the fifth largest city in India, has an estimated population of 14 million spread over 821 sq. 
km. Vehicle growth rates exceed 15% annually, compared to a population growth rate of 5%. The city’s 
radial road network comprises six National Highways and five State Highways, supplemented by 
circumferential roads such as the Inner Ring Road, Intermediate Ring Road, Outer Ring Road, West of 
Chord Road, NICE Corridor, Airport Road, and the Peripheral Ring Road (under construction). 
Recognizing the gravity of the situation, the Government of Karnataka, under the Greater Bengaluru 
Authority (GBA), has established Bengaluru Smart Infrastructure Limited (B-SMILE), a Special 
Purpose Vehicle (SPV) to implement mega infrastructure projects. These include: 
•  Underground Vehicular Tunnels: North–South Corridor (Hebbala to Silk Board, 18 km) and East–
West Corridor (K.R. Puram to Nayandahalli, 24 km). 
•  Elevated Flyover Corridors & Double-Decker Flyovers: 120 km. 
•  White topping (PQC) of Major Roads: 350 km. 
•  Road Widening Projects: Over 50 km within the city. 
•  Buffer Zone Road Construction: More than 300 km along lakes and storm water drains. 
These initiatives, including the proposed Elevated Corridor Project, are critical to addressing 
Bengaluru’s escalating traffic congestion, improving mobility, and sustaining economic growth. 
1.1 Background 
Bengaluru, historically founded by Kempe Gowda, a feudatory of the Vijayanagara Empire, was once 
renowned for its lush greenery and abundant water bodies. In the 1970s, the city enjoyed a green cover 
of nearly 68%, with splendid lakes, numerous parks, and tree-lined avenues defining its landscape. Until 
that period, Bengaluru’s economy was primarily driven by the manufacturing sector. Post-Independence, 
preferential policies of the Government of India further strengthened the city’s economic base, with 
several Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) establishing their headquarters here. The presence of reputed 
universities and colleges added to its stature as an educational hub. 
The State Government’s initiatives in the Information Technology sector, such as the establishment of 
the Karnataka State Electronics Development Corporation (Keonics) and the creation of Electronics 
City, provided a major boost to industrial growth. The availability of quality education, coupled with the 
emergence of new industries, created vast opportunities for the youth, leading to large-scale migration 
into the city. This sudden influx posed challenges for the Bengaluru Mahanagara Palike, which struggled 
to meet the growing demands. Numerous unplanned revenue layouts emerged across the city, many of 
which fell outside the jurisdiction of the Municipal Corporation and were consequently neglected in 
terms of infrastructure and services. 

=== PAGE 11 ===
 
5 
 
Bengaluru’s success in attracting large-scale private investment from across the globe further accelerated 
its growth trajectory. Between 1993 and 2004, while India’s GDP grew at an average rate of 7.93%, 
Bengaluru recorded an impressive 20.76% growth rate. The city’s IT sector, contributing nearly 40% of 
India’s software exports, played a pivotal role in bridging the nation’s trade deficit and cementing 
Bengaluru’s reputation as the “Silicon Valley of India.” 
However, this remarkable growth story has been accompanied by significant infrastructure-related 
challenges. Traffic congestion, deteriorating air quality, poor waste management, water scarcity, and 
inadequate road maintenance have adversely impacted the quality of life in the city. These issues have 
also affected the image of “Brand Bengaluru,” underscoring the urgent need for sustainable 
infrastructure solutions. The proposed Elevated Corridor Project from Sirsi Circle to Nayandahalli is one 
such initiative aimed at addressing these challenges by improving mobility, reducing congestion, and 
supporting the city’s continued economic growth. 
1.2 Development of Bengaluru 
Bengaluru, with a current population of approximately 14 million, has been growing at an annual rate of 
3.25% over the last decade. This rapid urban expansion has been accompanied by a phenomenal increase 
in vehicle ownership, particularly two-wheelers and four-wheelers, driven by rising household incomes. 
The number of registered motor vehicles has already crossed 12 million, placing immense pressure on 
the city’s limited road network. 
In the absence of an adequately developed public transport system, citizens have increasingly relied on 
personalized modes of travel. This has resulted in severe traffic congestion, escalating environmental 
pollution, and declining urban mobility. Studies indicate that an average citizen of Bengaluru spends 
more than 240 hours annually stuck in traffic. Such delays translate into significant productivity losses, 
deterioration of air quality, reduced quality of life, and increased costs for goods and services. 
Traffic composition on Bengaluru’s roads reveals a disproportionately high share of two-wheelers, with 
the share of cars also rising steadily. This trend underscores the inadequacy of the existing public 
transport system. Volume-to-Capacity (V/C) ratios on most arterial roads exceed 1.0, indicating 
oversaturation. The overall average traffic speed during peak hours is approximately 13.5 kmph, 
reflecting severe congestion and inefficiency in the transport network. 
These conditions highlight the urgent need to augment road capacity while simultaneously planning and 
implementing high-capacity mass transit systems along critical corridors. The proposed Elevated 
Corridor Project from Old Madras road from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station to Silk Board Junction 
on Hosur road is a strategic intervention aimed at addressing these challenges, improving mobility, and 
supporting the sustainable growth of Bengaluru. 

=== PAGE 12 ===
 
6 
 
1.3 Project road 
The proposed corridor from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station to Silk Board Junction, via Ejipura –
Koramangala–Madiwala–HSR Layout, falls within the Koramangala Planning District, located south of 
Bengaluru’s Central Business District. This district is characterized predominantly by residential areas 
such as Koramangala Layout and HSR Layout, along with public, semi-public, and unclassified land 
uses. 
The stretch from Madiwala to Old Madras Road via Domlur serves as one of Bengaluru’s major traffic 
corridors. It connects South and East Bengaluru, passing through key commercial and residential hubs 
including Madiwala, Koramangala, Domlur, and Indiranagar, before reaching Old Madras Road. This 
corridor experiences severe congestion, particularly during peak hours, due to several contributing 
factors: 
Key Factors Driving Congestion 
1. High Volume of Commuter and Commercial Traffic 
The corridor caters to a large number of office-goers, linking major business hubs in Koramangala, 
Domlur, and Indiranagar. With numerous IT companies, commercial establishments, and retail outlets 
along the route, traffic remains consistently heavy, peaking during morning and evening commute hours. 
2. Stop-and-Go Movement 
Heavy traffic volumes, frequent signals, and pedestrian crossings result in stop-and-go movement 
throughout the stretch. This pattern is especially evident near major intersections such as Sony World 
Junction and the Domlur Flyover. 
3. Peak Hour Delays 
Congestion is most severe between 8–11 a.m. and 5–8 p.m. Travel times increase significantly on 
Fridays and during rainy weather, when traffic slows further. 
Potential Interventions 
•  Improved Signal Coordination 
Deploying adaptive traffic signals that respond to real-time traffic conditions could ease congestion, 
particularly at critical intersections like Sony World Junction and Domlur Flyover. 
 
•  Expansion of Metro Connectivity 
Extending metro services along this corridor would provide a reliable alternative to private vehicles, 
reducing road traffic volumes. 

=== PAGE 13 ===
 
7 
 
•  Elevated Corridor Benefits 
By diverting through-traffic to an elevated structure, surface-level congestion can be reduced. This 
would minimize idling time, improve fuel efficiency, and lower emissions, thereby enhancing air quality 
in densely populated areas such as Koramangala and Domlur. 
1.4 Development of Project Road 
1. The Koramangala Planning District lies within the inner core of Bengaluru. It covers an area of 
3,083.02 hectares (30.83 sq. km) and comprises nine BBMP wards: 
a) Bangalore East Zone: Agaram (Ward 114), Neelasandra (116), Shanthi Nagar (117), Vannarpet 
(115) 
b) Bangalore South Zone: Adugodi (147), Ejipura (148), Koramangala (151), Jakkasandra (173) 
c) Bommanahalli Zone: HSR Layout (174) 
2. Land Use and Employment 
a) The district’s major employment base is commercial, encompassing both retail and office spaces. 
b) Small clusters of industrial activity exist in Adugodi, Ejipura, and Koramangala wards. 
c) Prominent markets and commercial centers include the Koramangala BDA Complex, Sony 
World Junction, Madiwala Market, and HSR Layout BDA Complex. 
3. Economic and Social Profile 
Koramangala and its surrounding neighborhoods have evolved into a vibrant start-up hub, attracting IT 
offices and entrepreneurial clusters due to their proximity to the Central Business District and Electronic 
City. The area also hosts a large number of restaurants, eateries, and retail outlets, with commercial 
development concentrated along several arterial roads. 
4. Traffic and Connectivity 
Traffic congestion is a pressing issue in this district, with severe bottlenecks at: 
a) Silk Board Junction 
b) Madiwala Check Post 
c) Ejipura 
d) Shoolay Circle 
e) Sony World Junction 
Despite these challenges, the district is well-served by BMTC bus services. The upcoming Phase 2 Metro 
along Hosur Road and the Outer Ring Road is expected to significantly strengthen the public transport 
network and ease congestion. Proposed metro stations within the district include: 
•  Hosur Road Corridor: Silk Board, HSR Layout 
•  Outer Ring Road Corridor: Venkatapura, Agara Lake, Ibbaluru 

=== PAGE 14 ===
 
8 
 
1.5 Proposal for Project Road Development 
The project under consideration involves the construction of an Elevated Corridor from Old Madras 
Road (Swami Vivekananda Metro Station) to Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road, passing through 
Indiranagar, Domlur, and Madiwala. This corridor is a critical link, facilitating traffic flow between the 
eastern and southern parts of Bengaluru. 
Existing Traffic Conditions 
a. Travel Speeds: Average vehicular speeds along this corridor range between 20–25 km/h throughout 
the day, with frequent traffic jams. 
b. Junction Delays: The corridor includes about three major junctions, where waiting times and vehicle 
queues during peak hours are particularly high. 
c. Pedestrian Activity: Pedestrian volumes are significant along the project road, especially near areas 
with dense commercial activity, further contributing to delays and safety concerns. 
Need for Intervention 
To ensure the free flow of through-traffic along this vital route, it is necessary to construct an elevated 
corridor. This intervention will: 
a. Relieve congestion at the surface level. 
b. Reduce delays at junctions. 
c. Improve overall travel efficiency and safety for both vehicles and pedestrians. 
1.6 Project Location and Salient Features 
The proposed project road, extending from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station on Old Madras Road 
to Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road, is situated in the southeastern part of Bengaluru. The 
location of the project road is illustrated in Figure 1-1 below.

Benefits of this Project 
a) Reduced Travel Time & Delays – Faster and more reliable movement along the corridor. 
b) Congestion Relief – Diverts through traffic above grade, easing surface-level pressure. 
c) Improved Level of Service – Enhances efficiency and comfort for commuters. 
d) Lower Fuel Consumption & Pollution – Reduced idling and stop‑and‑go movement improve air 
quality. 
e) Reduced Vehicle Operating Costs – Less wear and tear, lower maintenance expenses. 
f) Minimized Junction Delays & Conflicts – Smooth flow across critical intersections. 
g) Enhanced Connectivity – Better access to adjacent residential, commercial, and transit hubs. 
h) Capacity for Future Demand – Designed to accommodate projected traffic growth. 
i) Urban Regeneration Opportunities – Supports redevelopment and revitalization of surrounding 
areas. 
j) Transit-Friendly Environment – Encourages integration with sustainable public 
     Transport. 
k) Seamless Multimodal Transfers – Easier shift between metro, bus, and private modes. 

=== PAGE 26 ===
 
20 
 
l) Commercial Potential – Unlocks land value capture and integrates existing 
Retail/commercial clusters. 
m) Redevelopment Integration – Ensures rehabilitation or relocation of existing uses within the 
scheme.

of Swamy Vivekananda Metro Station on Old Madras Road passing through CMH Hospital, 100 Feet 
Road on Indiranagara, Domlur, Ejipura, Sony World Signal, Sarjapura Road, Madiwala connecting Silk 
Board Junction and Integrating existing elevated corridor (Electronic City Flyover at Begur Junction).

1.7.2 Seismic Analysis 
Seismic Zone: The project site is falling within a Seismically Active Region Zone II (Least activity 
zone). 
 
Figure 2-3 Seismic Zone Map 
 

=== PAGE 34 ===
 
28 
 
A seismic study would involve investigating the ground's response to seismic waves, which can be 
used to assess the potential for earthquake damage and guide construction in the area. While Bangalore 
falls under Seismic Zone II, meaning moderate earthquake risk, a seismic study can help identify areas 
with higher or lower risk within proposed project corridor.  
Seismic Refraction Analysis: 
 
This study use artificial seismic waves to map the subsurface layers and their properties.  
 Analyzing how these waves travel through the ground, the study can determine the depth and 
characteristics of soil and rock layers, which can affect how they respond to earthquakes.  
 This information can be crucial for understanding the potential for ground shaking and liquefaction 
in different parts of Proposed Project corridor.  
Seismic Micro Zonation Analysis 
 Seismic micro zonation involves dividing an area into smaller zones based on factors like soil type, 
topography, and geological structure. 
 Study helps to identify areas within Proposed Project corridor that might be more vulnerable to 
earthquake damage than others.  
 For instances, areas with soft soil or unstable slopes might be identified as higher risk zones.  
 
Ground Motion Analysis 
 This studies analyze how seismic waves propagate through the ground and how they affect 
buildings and structures. 
 This helps to estimate the ground shaking intensity in different parts of Proposed Project corridor 
during an earthquake.  
 The results of these studies can be used to develop building codes and construction guidelines that 
are appropriate for the local seismic conditions.  
Necessity of Seismic Analysis 
 This seismic study can provide valuable information for making informed decisions about 
construction and infrastructure development in Proposed Project corridor.  
 Understanding the local seismic conditions, it is possible to mitigate the risks associated with 
earthquakes and ensure the safety of buildings and people.

1.8 Objective of the Project 
The key objectives of the study are as follows: 
Assessment of Need – Evaluate the requirement for elevated road infrastructure along the project 
corridor, identify prevailing traffic problems, and ensure seamless travel in terms of connectivity, 
safety, and convenience. 
Conceptual & Detailed Planning – Develop a detailed conceptual design and prepare the Detailed 
Project Report (DPR) to support the client in effective project implementation. 
Mobility Enhancement – Propose actionable measures and conceptual-level designs to improve 
mobility along major corridors. 
The elevated roads are being constructed along arterial routes in Bengaluru primarily to alleviate traffic 
congestion and improve overall mobility. By providing a separate, elevated roadway, these projects 
aim to segregate long-distance commuter traffic from local traffic, thereby enhancing overall corridor 
capacity. 
Additionally, elevated corridors can: 
•  Prioritize public transport movement. 
•  Encourage a shift towards sustainable mobility solutions. 
•  Improve travel reliability and safety. 
•  Support long-term urban growth and economic activity. 
The following sections provide a detailed overview of the advantages of constructing the elevated 
corridor from Sirsi Circle to Nayandahalli: 
1.8.1 Advantages of the Proposed Project 
a) Reduced Congestion 
Elevated corridors are designed to accommodate heavy traffic flows on sub‑arterial roads, 
particularly during peak hours, by providing additional capacity and enabling faster vehicle 
movement. 
b) Improved Public Transport 
The project aims to prioritize the movement of public transport vehicles, ensuring faster and more 
efficient services, thereby encouraging commuters to shift from private to public modes. 
c) Augmented Road Capacity 
Elevated roads add significant capacity to existing arterial corridors, allowing smoother traffic flow 
and reducing bottlenecks at critical junctions. 
d) Enhanced Safety 
By segregating traffic streams, elevated corridors reduce conflict points and minimize accident risks, 
creating a safer environment for both vehicles and pedestrians. 
e) Sustainable Mobility 

=== PAGE 39 ===
 
33 
 
Reduced reliance on private vehicles and improved accessibility to public transport contribute to a 
more sustainable and transit‑friendly urban transport system. 
f) Economic Growth 
Improved connectivity and reduced travel time stimulate economic activity, enhance productivity, 
and support regional growth. 
g) Environmental Benefits 
Better traffic flow and reduced idling lower fuel consumption, decrease emissions, and improve air 
quality along densely populated stretches. 
1.9 Tender for Design Consultancy Services 
The consultancy services for the preparation of the Detailed Project Report (DPR) for the Construction 
of Proposed Elevated Corridor from Old Madras Road from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station to 
Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road via Indiranagar –Domlur-  Madiwala.have been awarded to M/s 
Siddi Shakthi Projects. The scope of this consultancy is limited to DPR preparation and tender 
processing for civil construction works. 
It is important to note that this report is intended solely for conceptual planning and tender 
documentation purposes and shall not be referred to for detailed engineering or execution-level designs. 
1.10 Objective of the Study 
The primary objectives of the study are: 
1. Engineering Surveys & Investigations: Conduct necessary engineering surveys and investigations 
to develop an innovative corridor design. 
2. Optimal Land Utilization: Propose a scheme in line with Indian Road Standards, ensuring 
maximum use of available open land and minimizing acquisition of private property. 
3. Modern & Economical Design: Implement the project by adopting modern concepts of innovative 
structural design, with emphasis on speed of construction and cost efficiency. 
4. Cost Estimation & Tender Documentation: Prepare accurate cost estimates and comprehensive 
tender documents to facilitate project implementation. 
1.11 Project Scope 
The scope of the consultancy study involves the following: 
1) Conducting Joint site inspection along with Departmental Engineers for understanding the name of 
road, starting, ending location of the project road and Requirements of the project. 
 

=== PAGE 40 ===
 
34 
 
2) Conducting Detailed Topographical survey, implementing Drone Technology and preparation and 
submission of final Auto CADD format Drawing showing Existing Right of Way (EROW) and 
Proposed Right of Way (PROW). 
3) Review and study of Geotechnical Investigation. 
4) Conducting Traffic Survey 
5) Preparation of GAD for Project Road 
6) Preliminary Structural design as per IRC Standards, 
7) Preparation of Road alignment and design 
8) Preparation of Estimate as per States schedule of rates  
9) Preparation of Tender document for inviting tender for construction of Elevated road and related 
works. 
10) Coordinating with Sub-Urban Rail to finalize location of piers to minimize impact on rail 
movement. 
Detailed Project Report including above scope of work has been prepared as part of the scope. 
1.12 Broad Methodology 
The following broad methodology has been adopted in the present study to arrive at a viable solution 
for an innovative design at the railway crossing, with the objective of improving traffic flow from all 
directions: 
1.  Traffic Flow Analysis 
Study the existing traffic circulation and anticipate future traffic demand along the corridor. 
2.  Integration with Future Plans 
Review proposed developments such as Metro expansion, Suburban Railway planning, and BDA/GBA 
proposals to ensure coordinated planning. 
3.  Adoption of Innovative Technology 
Explore modern construction technologies, including rapid construction methods and recent 
advancements in pre‑cast structural systems, to minimize traffic disruption during execution. 
4.  Road Design Improvements 
Prepare conceptual designs aimed at enhancing traffic movement and reducing congestion. 
5.  Quantity & Cost Estimation 
Develop detailed quantity calculations and cost estimates for the widening and improvement scheme. 
The detailed methodology adopted for the project is illustrated in a line diagram (Figure 1-5) is shown 
below for better understanding.

2 Survey and Investigation 
 
2.1 General 
Various engineering surveys have been carried out for the proper planning and design of the Elevated 
Corridor at the proposed site. Following surveys have been carried out: 
• Topographical survey 
• Traffic Survey 
• Geotechnical investigations for foundations 
The consultants visited the site along with BSMILE authority accompanied by concerned Executive 
engineer and with his team to acquaint the site and to study the various site related constraints which 
should be kept in mind while preparing the various alternative solutions for improvement of junction. 
2.2 Reconnaissance Survey 
A Reconnaissance Survey is carried out which is an initial, broad assessment of a study area to gather 
preliminary information and identify potential areas of interest. It's a crucial first step in many fields, 
including engineering, geology, and environmental science, helping to determine feasibility and guide 
further investigation.  
The detailed ground reconnaissance and the primary task of the reconnaissance survey carried out are 
includes: 
 Topographical features of the area. 
 Physical features in the proposed road within and outside PROW. 
 Preliminary identification of traffic pattern. 
 Soil (textural classifications). 
 Type and extent of existing utility services along the alignment (within PROW). 
 Geology of study area. 
 Seismic Zone. 
 Collection of Storm Water Drain details along the project alignment. 
 Collection of Topo map of project alignment from Survey of India. 
 Collection of GTS Bench Mark details in project alignment. 
 Collection of rainfall and flood data. 
 Sources of construction material. 
 Site Photos. 
The data collected from the reconnaissance surveys were utilized for planning and programming the 
detailed surveys and investigations. All field studies, including the traffic surveys, were undertaken on 
the basis of information derived from reconnaissance survey. Details were taken at every 50m intervals 
along the existing road and proposed alignment and levels are established in the study area. Sufficient 
site photos were captured during site visit and annexed in this report 

=== PAGE 44 ===
 
38

features in and around the project road and to assess the catchment area for planning drainage network 
along the project road.  
2.2.2 GTS Bench marks 
The GTS bench marks stands for Great Trigonometrical Survey.  These benchmarks are permanently 
fixed reference points with known elevations, established by the Survey of India, and are used as a 
standard for surveying and mapping. They serve as crucial reference points for determining elevations 
and depths in various surveying projects, especially when establishing mean sea level as a datum.  
GTS Bench marks of Survey of India for obtaining Mean Sea Level (MSL)was identified in the project 
corridor and Digital Auto level was used all along the project corridor for establishing Bench Marks 
marked on the available permanent structure along the project corridor. The accuracy obtained in 
levelling survey is 12mm k where k is distance in km. All levels used in this project are based on 
Survey of India GTS Bench Mark. These Bench Marks are marked in the topographical survey 
drawing. A list of Bench Mark is prepared and shown Table 2-1below.

2.3 Topographical Survey 
The basic objective of the topographic survey was to collect the essential ground features of the 
proposed junction using Total Station to develop a Digital Terrain Model (DTM), to take care of design 
requirements of grade separated facility, identifying areas of restriction and their remedies. The data 
collected will result in the final design and is also used for the Computation of earthwork and other 
quantities required. The Picture showing the survey work carried out at site is shown in the Figure 
2-2below. 
 
 
 
 
 

=== PAGE 47 ===
 
41 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 2-2Topographical Survey at Project Road 

=== PAGE 48 ===
 
42 
 
2.3.1 Detailed Survey of Topographical Features 
Topographical survey was conducted to capture the site features using 1 second accuracy total station 
instrument, Digital Auto level equipment and DGPS. The following features were collected during 
topographical survey to prepare the map of the physical features of the area. 
Following existing features have been captured during the survey: 
 Building lines, type of buildings (shops or houses, number of stories), trees and Right of Way 
boundary if available at site by presence of boundary stones. 
 Road edges, centerline, shoulders/footpaths, median etc. 
 Identifying all religious places, its locations, boundary lines and clear dimensions of compound 
walls and entrances. 
 All service lines both above and below ground such as OFC cables, water and sewer pipes, gas 
pipes, electrical poles and cables, telephone poles and lines etc. 
 Location of traffic islands, median, rotaries, dividers etc. 
 Location of road side drains, clearly identifying the type (open/close), width of drain, including 
the beginning and end of drains. 
 Positions of transformers, mast, towers etc. 
 Apart from the above, the names of intersecting roads and other landmarks are also recorded 
and incorporated in the drawing. 
Topographic survey was carried out using Total Station of 5-sec accuracy for detailed mapping and 
with higher accuracy total station during the traversing (min 3 sec). As part of the survey, the following 
activities were carried out. 
Bench Marks: As first step of the survey, The GTS Bench mark locations were identified in the project 
corridor. Bench marks were marked at every 250m interval on available permanent structure along the 
project Road. The list of the Bench marks of the project corridor is described in this DPR in Page No. 
38 & 39. 
Cross – Sections: Cross sections along the road have been taken at every 10m interval in longitudinal 
direction for a minimum width of 15m or up to the building lines from the centerline of the existing 
carriageway on either side of the road. Cross section levels were taken at. 
• Centerline of existing carriageway and median edges. 
• Points between centerline and edge of carriageway. 
 

=== PAGE 49 ===
 
43 
 
• Shoulder/Footpath edges/carriageway edges. 
• Additional points at locations of change in ground/critical points. 
 
Longitudinal Section: Longitudinal section levels along the centerline were taken at every 25 m 
interval. Where curves or important features were encountered, this interval was suitably reduced. 
Cross sections points for the required width was taken corresponding to each point in the longitudinal 
section. 
Map Plotting: The existing features surveyed were directly imported into Computer Aided Software 
and the details of the same has been plotted and presented for ready reference.

Primary traffic surveys which involve classified volume counts at selected sections along the project 
corridor to carrying out classified vehicle counts, with appropriate vehicle classification was adopted 
as per relevant guidelines of IRC keeping in mind the presence of different vehicle categories operating 
on the project road. The equivalent Passenger Car Unit (PCU) values was used as per relevant IRC 
guidelines. 
The traffic volume count surveys were conducted on 25th April 2025 as per IRC: 106-1990 guild lines, 
for Fourteen hours from morning 6 am to evening 8 pm to obtain classified count at the Project road. 
The data was collected on working day by deploying enumerators to record data for each hour within

2.5 Geo Technical Investigations 
Introduction  
Geotechnical investigation for the proposed project from Old Madras road to Silk Board via Indira 
Nagara 100 Feet Road, Domlur and Ejipura. The total length of the corridor is 11.625 km. The 
primary objective of this investigation is to establish the geotechnical condition at the site and to 
evaluate the allowable bearing pressure and other engineering design parameters through the various 
field and laboratory tests. The report consists of the details on the field and laboratory tests and the 
recommendations made based on the test results. 
Field work comprising of drilling of boreholes conducting SPT tests, collection of samples. The 
primary objective of this investigation was to obtain information about the sub-surface conditions 
at the site and obtain net allowable bearing pressure for design of foundations. The location of 
boreholes for the proposed construction site are marked in the topographical survey map annexed 
in this report. The Details are summarized below:

Boring and Drilling 
The field investigation comprised of advancing 150mm boreholes using rotary drilling rig with 
bentonite mud circulation. Standard penetration test (SPT) was conducted at every 1.50m intervals as 
per IS: 2131. The number of blows for 30cm penetration of split spoon sampler was recorded as N-
values. The boreholes were terminated after drilling to their respective depth. The various sub-surface 
strata are presented in the respective bore charts. 
 
Standard Penetration Test (SPT) in boreholes 
Standard Penetration Test (SPT) to determine penetration resistance was conducted in the boreholes 
using the procedure described in IS: 2131. In this method, driving bit is replaced by split spoon sampler 
(50.8 mm OD and 35 mm ID) and the sampler is driven by dropping 63.5 kg hammer on the top of the 
driving collar with a free fall of 75 cm. The length of the sampler is 60 cm. The sampler is first driven 
through 15 cm as “Seating Drive”. It is further driven through 30 cm. The number of blows required 
to drive the sampler for 30 cm beyond seating drive is termed as “Penetration Resistance, N”. 
Representative samples were collected using split spoon sampler. Where full 30cm penetration beyond 
seating drive was not possible, number of blows and corresponding penetration is mentioned in bore 
log sheets. 
 
Sample collections 
Samples were collected from split spoon sampler used for conducting SPT at close intervals of 1.5m 
up to end of respective strata. Rock formations are usually drilled by tungsten carbide core bits, core 
samples were retained and collected. Very dense strata being encountered at shallow depths, UDS 
Sampling as per IS: 2132 was limited in upper depths. Samples were collected from split spoon sampler 
used in SPT done at 2.0m intervals as per IS:2131. Undisturbed sampling was limited since very dense 
strata is noticed from shallow depths itself in most of the boreholes with N Values generally >30). 
 
Rock formations 
In boreholes where rock cores could be recovered, same was logged, placed sequentially in good 
quality wooden core boxes. Individual core piece lengths were measured and core recovery and Rock 
quality designation computed as below. 
 
Rock core recovery {CR % = (Length of Core / Length of run) x 100} Rock quality designation (RQD) 
= (Total length of core pieces of >100mm / length of run) x 100}. Same is reflected in bore logs. 
 

=== PAGE 55 ===
 
49 
 
Water Table 
During the time of investigations, subsequent to completion of borehole and after allowing water 
level to stabilize for minimum 24 hours, water table was not encountered in the explored depth 
below natural ground level during the time of investigation. However, water levels are invariably 
subjected to seasonal fluctuations. 
 
Laboratory Tests  
The following Tests were carried out  
• Grain size analysis  
• Natural Moisture content  
• Atterberg’s Limits  
 
 
Sub - Soil profile analysis 
Nature of Soil Stratification Based on detailed analysis, the soil conditions is described for the entire 
project corridor as under 
Layer I: Soil Overburden 
The sub-soil stratification essentially comprises of sandy Silt/silty Sand with clay binder formations 
with layer thickness of 9.50 – 19.00m. 
 
Layer II: Completely Weathered Rock  
Below layer I and comprises of ‘very dense’ complete to highly weathered rock (SPT ‘N’ values 
consistently N >100 and Nil core recovery). In ‘in-situ’ conditions, stratum is considered ‘very 
dense/stiff’ /incompressible with very good bearing characteristics. In this layer sample recovery 
was limited and sufficient for visual classification.

Recommendations 
Based on the geotechnical investigations carried out for the proposed project, following recommendations are 
made: 
For shallow foundations 
1. Foundations for all the structures shall be taken minimum 1.5m below structural floor level. 
2. Isolated/combined footing up to minimum width 2m may be designed with the following 
allowable bearing pressure of, which gives a factor of safety of 3.0 against shear failure and for 
an allowable settlement of 25mm.

Note: 
1. During excavations, in case any variations in strata/seepage are noticed, the same shall be 
brought to the notice of geotechnical engineer for review of net SBC recommended. 
2. The bottom of foundation shall be properly levelled and verified for loose pockets/weaker zones 
and if found, the same shall be replaced with lean concrete. 
Recommendations for deep foundations 
It is recommended for RCC bored cast in-situ piles wherever deep foundations are envisaged. 
Inferences from borehole investigations 
Heavy load transfer inspected from the Bridge structure proposed. So deep foundations such as Bored 
Cast-in-situ Piles is recommended. Pile diameter has been considered as 1.2m. Piles should be socketed 
three times the Pile diameter in dense weathered rock/refusal strata (N>100) layer or 0.5 times the pile 
diameter in to Rock formations whichever is met earlier. 
Recommendations for Deep Foundations 
For deep foundations RCC bored cast in-situ piles is recommended. Design calculations are as per IRC 
78 approach Appendix V Method 2.

Note: 
 Concreted pile lengths are estimated based on Borehole data. Actual Length of piles may vary. 
During actual execution, pile termination shall be decided based on chiseling / PPR deduced by 
SPT ‘N’ value or any approved criteria. Broad description of PPR criteria related to SPT N is 
given below 
 
 Pile Termination reconfirmation-For reconfirming the strata at pile termination level (socket 
strata) Chiseling criteria or Pile Penetration Ratio recommended in IRC-78 may be used 
Pile Penetration ratio (PPR) reflects the energy in ton-m required to advance pile bore of 1m2 
cross sectional area by 1cm or 10mm. SPT Tests are used to assess this and minimum N Value 
/blow count of 100 may be used as a guideline. 
From SPT Test for N=100 (Indicative calculation)  
Energy E Spent for N blows =63.5kgx 75cm x N Blows  
Area of SPT Sampler = πD2/4 = 0.785x 25.81 =20.26cm2 
D =5.08 cm Outer diameter (Standard samplers)  
For N=100, Energy E = 63.5 x 75x100x 10-5 t-m 
Conclusion 
The scope of the geotechnical investigation was limited to evaluating the nature and type of sub‑soil 
strata, determining foundation design parameters, and identifying the type and depth of sub‑structure 
foundations at the project site. The recommendations provided are based on the limited field and 
laboratory tests carried out during this stage. 
During actual excavation for construction, further recommendations and appropriate modifications 
may be required. Any discrepancies observed between site conditions and this report should be 
immediately brought to the notice of the project consultant, who will provide suitable guidance.

3 Methodology and Design Standards 
3.1 General 
After the study of the Traffic survey details and Interference at the project road, an arrangement best 
suiting to the traffic pattern is proposed for improving the traffic situation. Proposal is evolved giving 
due consideration to minimize land acquisition. All the site constraints have been taken care while 
formulating the improvement scheme. 
Efforts have been made to create a conflict-free situation within the possible limits of the right of way, 
the main objective of which is to greatly improve the current situation and expand the flow of traffic 
as much as possible.  
Current and future traffic growth pattern, location of bus stops, intersection, environmental impact etc. 
are considered. Thereafter, a traffic survey to estimate the future population of traffic is conducted in 
order to determine the number of lanes in each direction. The planned layout of the flyover is carefully 
chosen to avoid disturbance to traffic and maximize the decongestion potential. 
3.2 Study of Existing Project Road 
The Project road has been studied and the starting point of the project road is a diversion from the 
busiest road of National Highway No.-44 which is part of the arterial city road. 
K.R. Puram, also known as Krishnarajapuram, is a prominent locality in Bengaluru, located in the 
eastern part of the city on National Highway 44 (NH-44). Specifically, the Peenya – Hebbal-K.R. 
Puram-Hosur road, is a part of NH-44. Traffic flow on this road turn onto Project Road at Swami 
Vivekananda Metro Station and proceed to Hosur Road via Silk Board Junction.   
Added to the above, the Hebbel flyover in Bengaluru is part of the National Highway 7 (NH 7), 
specifically at the intersection of NH 7 and the Outer Ring Road (ORR). It is a crucial junction for 
traffic entering and exiting the city from the north. This traffic also connecting to the Project Road on 
the northeast through the ORR to lead towards Hosur. 
After analyzing the traffic and road inventory survey of the project road, the improvement proposal 
was considered and a detailed study was carried out to suggest a suitable concept of proposal for the 
elevated road. 
3.3 Augmentation of Traffic capacity 
The proposed project road, extending from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station on Old Madras 
Road to Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road via Indiranagar–Domlur–Ejipura–Koramangala–
Madiwala, is located in the southeastern part of Bengaluru, close to the Central Business District 
(CBD). 
It is surrounded by well-established localities: 
a) West: Ulsoor 

=== PAGE 97 ===
 
91 
 
b) South: Domlur 
c) East: Vimanapura 
d) North: Baiyappanahalli 
Situated in the heart of the city, Indiranagar enjoys strong connectivity through Old Madras Road, 
the Namma Metro, and southern links to Ejipura, Koramangala, and Silk Board towards Hosur Road. 
Need for Capacity Augmentation 
a) The CBD and adjoining areas face high traffic demand, requiring augmentation of arterial 
road capacity. 
b) Indiranagar exhibits one of the more balanced road densities in the city, making it a strategic 
corridor for intervention. 
c) While at-grade augmentation is the default option, land acquisition constraints along the 
project road necessitate consideration of elevated road solutions. 
Strategic Importance of the Elevated Corridor 
a) Vehicles from Hebbal via ORR, Baiyappanahalli Railway Terminal, KR Puram via Old 
Madras Road, and Halasuru/MG Road can directly access Silk Board Junction once the 
elevated corridor is implemented. 
b) A flyover connecting Indiranagar to Silk Board via Ejipura will address severe congestion 
on the Inner Ring Road. 
c) Current delays at signalized junctions significantly impact commuting time and fuel efficiency. 
d) The elevated corridor will provide a faster, more direct route, reducing travel time and 
improving traffic flow. 
Broader Impact 
Elevated corridors are not just local solutions; they serve as critical connectors across urban regions, 
linking different parts of the city and supporting national-level mobility. Such infrastructure projects 
act as pillars of urban and economic growth, strengthening both city-level and country-level 
development. 
 
 
 

=== PAGE 98 ===
 
92 
 
3.4 Structural type Options 
Depending on span length, space availability, construction speed requirements, traffic management 
constraints, machinery and material movement limitations, and environmental considerations, one of 
the following structural types is selected for elevated corridors after evaluating their advantages and 
disadvantages: 
i. 
Cast-in-situ on staging 
ii. 
Precast at site or in casting yard, then erected or launched 
iii. 
Segmental construction (cast-in-situ or precast) 
iv. 
Incremental launching (push-in method) 
For the superstructure, continuous spans are preferred as they reduce structural depth, improve riding 
quality and durability, and enhance aesthetics. Minimizing substructure depth also helps reduce the 
overall flyover length. 
Segmental construction is often adopted in urban situations to facilitate smooth traffic flow during 
construction and to accelerate project timelines. 
The following superstructure options were studied to meet IRC standards and arrive at the most 
suitable, economical, and site-specific solution: 
a)   Elevated road with precast I-girder 
b)   Elevated road with full-span precast box girder 
c)   Elevated road with segmental precast box girder 
d)   Elevated road with UHPFRC U-girder 
3.4.1 Elevated Road with Pre-cast I- Girder. 
Elevated roads using precast I‑girders and box girders offer advantages such as faster construction, 
reduced on‑site labor, and improved quality control. I‑girders, with their simple I‑shaped form, are 
economical and lighter for shorter spans. However, they have limited torsional stiffness, require deeper 
sections as spans increase, and involve cumbersome transport and launching operations. These factors 
can lead to longer construction timelines and higher substructure costs when applied to extended urban 
corridors. For this reason, while I‑girders remain suitable for limited span applications, modern 
alternatives such as precast PSC box girders, double‑T girders, or UHPFRC girders are generally 
preferred for elevated roads, as they provide greater efficiency, durability, and speed of construction. 

=== PAGE 99 ===
 
93 
 
3.4.2 Elevated road with Box Girder with full span precast 
Simple box girders are efficient structural forms for spans up to 60 m, whether in simply supported or 
continuous construction. They also have wide applicability for longer spans, particularly when 
segmental construction is adopted. 
Box Girder: A closed, hollow section with two flanges and two or more webs. 
a. 
Advantages: 
i. High torsional resistance due to the enclosed section. 
ii. Capable of spanning longer distances than I‑girders for the same depth. 
iii. Suitable for carrying larger loads and applications where torsional stiffness is critical. 
b.   Disadvantages: 
i.   More complex to precast and construct in full-span lengths compared to I‑girders. 
ii.   Heavier for the same load-bearing capacity, leading to higher costs. 
For time-bound mass rapid transit projects (metros, high-speed rail), full-span precast box girders are 
generally preferred over segmental precast box girders. They enable faster construction progress, 
minimize traffic disruption, and deliver superior quality since the entire girder is cast in one operation 
at ground level, avoiding segmental joints. 
However, transporting and launching full-span precast box girders is significantly more challenging 
than segmental girders, especially in congested urban environments. Constraints include limited space, 
traffic management, movement of heavy machinery and materials, and environmental considerations. 
Specialized equipment such as molds, straddle carriers, bridge gantries, launching girders, and girder 
transporters is required, increasing capital costs. Despite these costs, the method offers substantial 
benefits in speed and quality. 
In dense urban areas, bidder participation for full-span girder tenders is often limited due to traffic 
congestion and logistical difficulties. Considering these challenges, the full-span precast box girder 
option was not selected for this project.  The photo showing launching of full span box girder is shown 
in Figure 3-1below. 

=== PAGE 100 ===
 
94 
 
 
Figure 3-1 Launching of Full span Box girder 
3.4.3 Elevated road with Segmental pre-cast Box girder. 
Full‑span girders are designed to be launched in one piece, enabling faster construction progress, while 
segmental girders are built in multiple precast sections and are more adaptable to complex geometries. 
The project road includes both straight and curved alignments, requiring careful design to ensure safety, 
comfort, and efficiency. A segmental box girder is particularly suitable for such geometry, as it offers 
advantages in handling combinations of straight and curved spans. Segmental girders are constructed 
from multiple precast segments joined together at the project site, whereas full‑span girders are cast as 
a single continuous piece for the entire span. 
Urban environments often present challenges for flyover construction, including traffic disruption, 
space limitations, and the need to work around existing infrastructure. Segmental precast box girders 
are well suited to overcome these constraints. They are widely adopted for urban flyovers due to their 
efficiency, speed of construction, and ability to accommodate complex geometries. In addition, they 
minimize traffic disruption, provide high quality control, and ensure long‑term durability, making them 
an ideal choice for densely populated areas. A launching photo of the segmental pre-cast girder is 
shown in Figure 3-2 below. 

=== PAGE 101 ===
 
95 
 
 
Figure 3-2 Launching of Segmental elements of Box Girder 
3.4.3.1 Segmental Box Girder Concept 
A segmental box girder flyover is a bridge structure where hollow box-shaped girders are constructed 
from precast or cast-in-situ concrete segments, connected and post-tensioned to form a continuous 
superstructure. This method combines the strength and torsional resistance of box girders with the 
efficiency and adaptability of segmental construction, making it suitable for long spans and complex 
alignments. 
For wide decks exceeding 15 m, single-cell box girders with struts supporting the deck cantilevers, or 
with internal struts to reduce slab spans, are preferred. These configurations avoid the need for multi-
cell box girders, which are more difficult to construct. Such geometry is particularly encouraged in the 
design of the proposed 17.2 m wide elevated flyover. 
As span length increases, girder depth also increases, requiring larger substructures and foundations. 
This leads to longer construction timelines and higher costs. In urban environments, where traffic 
growth and space constraints demand rapid solutions without land acquisition, segmental box girders 
provide efficiency and adaptability. 
Recent advances in Ultra-High Performance Fiber-Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) technology allow 
spans of 70–100 m to be precast in thinner sections, reducing self-weight and minimizing substructure 
requirements. This results in faster construction and cost savings. While UHPFRC technology has been 
introduced in India and encouraged at the DPR stage, its adoption is still emerging. 
Considering the site conditions and constraints of this project, the use of segmental precast box girders 
is not recommended at this juncture, though UHPFRC remains a promising option for future 
applications.

3.4.3.2 Ultra – High Performance Fiber- Reinforced Concrete U Girder 
As Bengaluru Smart Infrastructure Limited (B‑SMILE) advances towards building high-speed 
corridors with shorter implementation timelines, innovation and modernization are essential. 
Ultra‑High Performance Concrete (UHPC) offers an ideal solution to this challenge. With compressive 
strengths ranging from 150 to 250 MPa, UHPC provides exceptional durability and mechanical 
performance, enabling the production of structural components that are lightweight and slender in 
section. 
These qualities allow UHPC systems to be adopted more widely in elevated infrastructure, reducing 
dead load and improving efficiency. Compared to conventional cast‑in‑situ construction or precast 
systems using normal high‑strength concrete, UHPC enables faster transportation and erection of 
longer precast elements with lower energy requirements. 
The use of UHPC can also result in slimmer bridge piers than those required with conventional 
concrete, accelerating construction and freeing valuable ground space in metropolitan areas. This is 
particularly advantageous for elevated road projects, as it increases carriageway space and minimizes 
disruption to existing traffic 
3.4.3.3 Making process of UHPFRC 
The performance of Ultra‑High Performance Fiber‑Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) depends on 
achieving low porosity and optimized particle packing. Reducing porosity increases strength, 
durability, and resistance to environmental degradation. This is accomplished through: 
a. Close packing of raw materials to minimize voids. 
b. •  Use of super plasticizers (HRWR additives) to reduce water demand while maintaining 
workability. 
c. •  Incorporation of finely ground reactive mineral admixtures to enhance density and 
performance. 
d. •  Low water‑to‑binder ratio (w/b), which is critical for achieving high strength and durability. 

=== PAGE 106 ===
 
100 
 
e. Composition: 
f. •  Cement content typically ranges from 800–1000 kg/m³, which provides very high strength 
but increases heat of hydration, shrinkage risk, and production costs. 
g. •  Fine aggregates are used with particle sizes between 150–800 µm. 
h. •  Inert fillers such as quartz powder (0.1–100 µm) improve packing density. 
i. •  Supplementary cementitious materials (SCMs) can reduce costs while maintaining 
performance. 
 
 
Steel Fibers: 
Steel fibers are added to improve ductility, toughness, and crack resistance. Optimal fiber dosage is 
essential — concentrations above 2% by volume provide limited additional benefit due to difficulties 
in uniform dispersion. 
Mixing Process: 
The mixing procedure must be carefully controlled to achieve uniformity and performance. Key 
considerations include: 
a.   Proper sequence of adding materials. 
b. •  Dry mixing of binders, fillers, and aggregates before introducing water and HRWR       
        additives. 
c. •  Control of mixing time (typically 15–20 minutes), speed, and temperature. 
d. •  Ensuring homogeneous dispersion of fibers throughout the matrix. 
3.4.3.4 Mix Proportioning and Mechanical Characterisation 
It is important to note that Ultra-High Performance Concrete (UHPC) does not contain coarse 
aggregates. Instead, it relies on fine powders and fibers to achieve very high strength and durability. A 
typical proprietary UHPC mix is shown in the Figure 3-3 and Figure 3-4 below:

=== PAGE 112 ===
 
106 
 
UHPFRC (Ultra‑High‑Performance Fiber‑Reinforced Concrete) is an emerging material in bridge and 
flyover construction, valued for its superior mechanical properties and durability compared to 
conventional concrete. When combined with a strutted box girder design, UHPFRC offers several 
advantages: increased strength and stiffness, enhanced service life, potential for longer spans, improved 
aesthetics, and reduced construction time. 
 
For this project, a UHPFRC U‑box girder has been adopted for the 8.5 m carriageway, requiring a pier 
cap size of 4.5m. For the 17.2 m carriageway, however, a UHPFRC strutted box girder has been selected, 
which accommodates the same width with a pier cap size of only 6.5 m effectively halving the pier cap 
length compared to a U‑box girder. 
 
Due to its extremely high compressive and flexural strength, UHPC allows for smaller cross‑sections with 
significantly reduced dead weight. Although UHPC is denser than conventional concrete, its flexural 
strength is several times higher, enabling lighter overall structures. Higher modulus values also mean 
reduced deflections compared to CC or HPC. 
 
A comparative study shows that a 30 m span, 17.2 m wide segmental precast box girder requires a depth 
of 2.2 m, while a 45 m span, 17.2 m wide UHPFRC U‑girder achieves the same depth of 2.2 m. This 
demonstrates that UHPC enables longer spans without increasing girder depth. The resulting reduction in 
dead weight proportionally decreases forces on the substructure, leading to savings in foundation size and 
cost. 
 
Recommendation: For longer spans in urban flyovers, UHPFRC strutted box girders provide an ideal 
solution, combining efficiency, durability, and economy while minimizing substructure requirements. 
3.4.3.5 Limited use of UHPFRC 
Despite its many advantages, Ultra‑High Performance Fiber‑Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) has limited 
application in India at present. The reasons include: 
a) High Cost and Proprietary Supply 
i. UHPC is currently available in India only as a proprietary product, supplied  by a few firms 
in   
    pre‑packed bags ready for mixing. 
ii. 
This restricted supply chain results in very high costs, presently around ₹1,00,000 per cubic 
metre, making widespread adoption economically challenging. 
b) Lack of National Design Standards 

=== PAGE 113 ===
 
107 
 
i. While countries such as France and Switzerland have developed detailed codes            covering 
UHPC material specifications, testing, execution, and design standards, India has yet to establish 
comprehensive guidelines. 
ii. 
The absence of Indian Standards (IS) or IRC‑based design provisions is a major hindrance, 
as engineers and contractors lack formal references for design, quality control, and 
compliance. 
3.4.3.6 NHAI Notification for Use of UHPFRC Structural elements  
The National Highways Authority of India (NHAI), through Circular No. RW/NH‑33049/01/2020 : S&R 
(B) Pt dated 22 February 2022, has emphasized the use of UHPFRC girders for longer spans of 50–100 m, 
compared to traditional girder systems typically limited to spans of around 30 m. 
The Authority has recognized several advantages of UHPFRC girders: 
a) Faster construction timelines. 
b) Substantial savings in substructures and foundations. 
c) Longer durability and service life. 
d) Reduction in carbon footprint when applied to longer spans. 
To promote adoption, the Government has permitted consultants to introduce modern UHPC structural 
elements in upcoming DPRs, particularly for National Highway viaduct projects exceeding 2000 m in 
length in urban areas, subject to evaluation of economic implications. 
Furthermore, the Government has relaxed the application of conventional Indian codes for UHPFRC until 
specific IS/IRC standards are published. Concessionaires and EPC contractors are allowed to incorporate 
UHPFRC technology even in ongoing projects tendered prior to the circular, where conventional precast 
systems were originally specified. 
Based on this national policy direction, the State Government can confidently align with the same 
approach, ensuring the best use of advanced technology for faster, more durable, and sustainable elevated 
infrastructure. 
3.5 Recommendation of Structural elements for super structure 
In order to achieve longer span bridges, Ultra‑High Performance Fiber‑Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) 
has proven effective in meeting design requirements compared to conventional reinforced concrete 
systems. By enabling spans of 50–100 m, UHPFRC reduces the number of foundations required. Since 
foundation works are among the most time‑consuming activities in any project, this reduction provides a 
significant advantage in terms of construction speed and overall project delivery. 
 

=== PAGE 114 ===
 
108 
 
With these benefits—fewer foundations, faster construction, improved durability, and reduced carbon 
footprint—combined with the guidance of the NHAI Circular (22 February 2022) encouraging the use of 
UHPFRC girders, it is recommended that this methodology be adopted for the elevated corridor project. 
Accordingly, UHPFRC girders have been selected for implementation in this project.

to evaluate potential savings in both cost and construction time. The analysis clearly demonstrates that 
adopting UHPFRC girders results in significant advantages over conventional alternatives.

3.6 Design Standards 
3.6.1 Planning of Geometry and Structural Design Standards 
Planning criteria for elevated flyovers, as outlined by the Indian Road Congress (IRC), 
include considerations for traffic analysis, design speed, road width, and vertical clearance. These criteria 
ensure safe and efficient traffic flow and adhere to standards for urban areas.  
3.6.2 Geometry 
The objective of geometric design is to provide maximum efficiency in traffic operations and maximum 
safety at reasonable cost. The horizontal geometry shall be designed in accordance with IRC 86 -
1983.However, the Geometry in urban areas mainly depends on site conditions like modifying geometry 
of alignment at existing structure, to maintain existing road profile, restriction of width due to building 
lines etc., 
3.6.3 Carriageway Width 
i. The carriageway width for four lane shall be provided as per the provisions of IRC Codes for the 
traffic requirements as determined by traffic census and studies.

=== PAGE 118 ===
 
112 
 
ii. The carriageway width on the elevated structure shall be kept same as on the approaching roads 
on both side and the widths shall not be reduced abruptly under any circumstances. 
3.6.4 Design Speed 
Design speed is related to the function of a road. Keeping in view the type of road considering for the 
project road as sub arterial/collector Street as per the urban road with mixed traffic system, the design 
speeds of 40kmph is adopted. 
3.6.5 Horizontal Curves 
Horizontal curves should consist of a circular portion flanked by spiral transitions at both end. Design 
speed, super-elevation and coefficient of side friction affect the design of circular curves. Length of 
transition curves is determined on the basis of rate of change of centrifugal acceleration and super-
elevation. Horizontal curves, are designed in accordance with I RC: 38 1988. 
3.6.6 Widening at Curves 
Wherever horizontal curves are provided in Grade separators, the carriageway is widened as per relevant 
codes. The structures having entry and exit ramps are provided with adequate length of 
acceleration/deceleration/merging lanes to ensure free and safe flow of traffic and to avoid congestion 
near the mouth of such ramps resulting in traffic jams.  
3.6.7 Sight Distance 
 Adequate stopping sight distance shall be provided at all points on the road in conformity with IRC 
standards for 40Kmph 60m Sight distance is proposed: 
3.6.8 Clearances at Elevated Structures 
The minimum horizontal clearance shall be the clear width and the minimum vertical clearance the clear 
height available for the passage of traffic. The minimum vertical clearance of any structure provided over 
the project road shall be 5.0 m for non-urban areas and 5.5 m in case of urban areas. In case of structures 
over local or collector streets, where only light commercial vehicles are plying and where alternative 
routes for heavy commercial vehicles and fire tenders are available within a short distance, this may relax 
the clearance requirements of 5.5m. At locations where over-dimensional vehicles are frequently plying, 
will consider providing increased vertical clearance. The minimum horizontal and vertical clearances for 
single and multiple lane bridges with vehicular traffic shall be followed as per the provisions of IRC: 5. 
3.6.9 Super-elevation 
Super-elevation shall be provided on a horizontal curvature in accordance with the relevant IRC Codes. 

=== PAGE 119 ===
 
113 
 
The effect of super elevation on stresses in the various members of the structure shall be taken into account 
in the design Super-elevation shall be limited to 7 percent. However, as the project road is in urban sections 
with frequent intersections, it may be limited to 4 percent. 
3.6.10 Gradient and Profile 
 The Gradients in the approaching viaducts or earth embankments shall usually be limited to as per the 
relevant provisions of IRC codes 92-1985. However, in situations where sufficient space or length is not 
available then the gradients may be locally increased to meet the requirement after judiciously considering 
the type of traffic using the facility. For vertical gradient for ramps will be limited to 4% as per site 
condition and will not be exceed 6%. 
3.6.11 Drainage 
Effective drainage shall be ensured by providing drainage spouts connected with horizontal and vertical 
pipe system such that the water from the structure does not fall on the roads, does not stagnate over the 
roads or at entry and exit points of the grade separators and is discharged into the overall draining system 
devised for the project area. 
3.6.12 Camber 
Camber shall be provided as per relevant IRC codes. Camber on grade separator with high type bituminous 
surfacing or cement concrete surfacing should be 1 .7 to 2 .5 percent. The camber of 2.5% is proposed in 
deck and approaches. 
 
3.6.13 Railings, Parapet or Crash Barriers 
Railings or parapets shall be provided on both sides of the bridge for the protection of traffic. Apart from 
its structural strength considerations shall be given primarily to the architectural features of the railing or 
parapet to obtain proper proportioning of its various elements to harmonies with the structure as a whole. 
Consideration shall also be given to avoid, as far as is possible obstruction of the view from the passing 
motor cars.  
Crash barriers shall be provided for grade separators designed for high speed vehicles particularly project 
roads in urban areas. Crash barrier of concrete is provided. 
3.7 Design Standards of Structures. 
Design of Elevated Corridor shall be carried out as per relevant IRC standards. 
3.7.1 Materials of Construction & Specifications 
The material for the grade separators is generally concrete. 

=== PAGE 120 ===
 
114 
 
One of the considerations in selecting the concrete material is that construction should be possible without 
causing too much inconvenience to the existing traffic for a long time. Use of Ultra High performance of 
Fiber reinforced Concrete of M150 to M 200 is proposed to make the structure look slim and more durable. 
3.7.2 Loads 
Loading standards shall be in accordance with IRC: 6. for grade separators of 2 lanes and above, live load 
categories as defined in IRC: 6 is adopted. 
3.7.2.1 Bumper to Bumper Traffic  
In crowded towns, vehicles have to stop at traffic signals or on account of traffic jams. In such cases 
vehicles may be standing bumper to bumper on grade separators. The effect of this loading should also be 
considered in the design. Proposed to design as per IRC-Class A vehicle. 
The crash barriers of concrete is proposed to be provided for grade separators designed for use by high 
speed vehicles in urban areas just after the ends of carriageway. The loading and design criteria for crash 
barriers shall be as per 1RC; 6. 
The foundations shall be designed to withstand the worst combination of loads and forces evaluated in 
accordance with the provisions of Clauses 700 and 707 of IRC: 78. The foundations shall be taken to such 
depths that they are sufficient from consideration of bearing capacity, settlement, stability and suitability 
of strata at the founding level and sufficient depth below it. 
The part of the grade separators falling in line of traffic will be designed for collision loads and impact as 
per IRC:78 
3.7.3 Minimum Dimensions 
The minimum dimensions of various members or components shall follow the provisions of relevant IRC 
Codes.Using the new technology of UHPFRC, the thickness of the structural components based on IRC 
loading depends on the actual design and is certified by the authority before implementation. 
3.7.4 Design for Continuity 
The continuity of the superstructure is achieved by providing full continuity or continuity for live load 
only. This is achieved by providing superstructure longitudinally continuous over intermediate supports 
on bearings. 
Segmental box girder bridges are typically designed with a U shaped box- cross-section, often using 
prestressed concrete, steel/steel fiber, or a combination of both. Geometric design considerations include 
span lengths, box dimensions (width, depth, and length of segments), and the arrangement of cells within 
the box. Structural design focuses on ensuring the bridge's stability, durability, and ability to withstand 
loads, considering factors like span-to-depth ratios, material properties, and connection details.

115

14 
Segmental Construction 
A typical segmental box girder is designed with a 
length of approximately 5.0 m and a depth of 2.2 m, 
excluding the deck slab thickness of 250 mm. The cross 
slope is generally provided at 2.5% to facilitate surface 
drainage. 
15 
Launching methodology 
1. Span‑by‑Span Method 
Each segment is placed sequentially and stressed span 
by span, ensuring controlled alignment and stability 
during construction. 
2.  Pre‑Assembly and Lifting Method 
After all segmental girders are assembled and stressed 
on the ground, a 150–200 MT capacity crane can lift a 
single girder unit and place it directly on the pier cap

3.8 Approaches to Elevated structures in Urban Locations 
 The project road is in urban areas sufficient width is not available and wider roads & drains are required 
on all sides. Earthen banks also require considerable quantity of earth which has to be brought from long 
distance & the cost is also more. Considerable costly land is occupied by the approaches. Solid earthen 
embankments also obstruct the view and do not look good. 
 As such earthen approaches are not considered in this project road. Viaduct spans up to a height of 5.0 m 
is extended so that in the last span a vertical clearance of 3 to 3.5 m is available. This may be followed by 
retained embankment either by retaining walls or reinforced earth walls. 
3.8.1 Retaining Walls and Return Walls 
Reinforced Soil Structures is proposed as Retaining wall and to protect the earth at the last span of viaduct 
to retain the earth. 
i.Reinforced earth retaining walls constructed with soil backfill reinforced with inextensible flexible 
reinforcement, such as metallic strips or sheets or extensible geo synthetic materials, such as; geo-
textiles and geo-grid, are used for solid ramp portion for approaches of flyovers, grade separators and 
or embankment. It does not cover load bearing abutments.  
ii.Selected fill and filler material conforming to specifications given in relevant IRC standards are used 
in the reinforced soil structure as back fill behind fascia panels. The material is granular and purely 
non-cohesive.  
iii.Geo-synthetic material may be woven, non-woven or knitted sheet used in geo-technical engineering 
and other civil engineering applications that sustain design loads at permissible strains and defined 
creep characteristic with maximum elongation less than the permissible limit.  
 
iv.Metallic reinforcement is in the form of strip or rod or square welded mesh/ net which sustains load at 
strains less than or equal to the permissible value. 
 
v.The facia panels should be cast with proper line and finish preferably in a casting yard. They can be 
given different treatment with patterns to suit the aesthetical requirements or giving any effect. 
 
vi.The back fill material should be properly compacted in the designed thickness of layers, as per the 
specifications.  
 
vii.The facia panels should be erected to give uniform surfaces and fixed to the reinforcing materials such 
that these are not dislodged.  
 
viii.The drainage layer of prescribed specifications should be provided as required by design and 
specifications and it should be ensured that it will remain functional and will not be choked because of 
flow of silt particles in the backfill, during the outflow of water, as it may result in the failure of 
embankment.

4.1 Elevated Corridor and approaches. 
Considering the present and feature growth of traffic volume and movement pattern to reduce the traffic 
congestion between the Old madras road and towards Silk board through Indiranagara, Domlur and 
Koramangala via Madivala an elevated corridor of four lane bidirectional flyover is proposed.

4.2 At Grade Road improvement 
The 4.430 km long at-grade road starts from the metro station on Old Madras Road to Domlur Junction 
is constructed within white topping and the buffer zone between the edge of the CC road and the sidewalk 
is paved with cement concrete pavers. The center of the road is bisected with a centerline of 0.5 m 
median. 
When an elevated structure is started for construction, the central section of 3 meters width is 
continuously barricaded and utilized for the construction of foundation and substructure works within 
the site. An additional 2 meters of space will be used in addition to the 3 meters at each pier location. 
During construction, at some places, the CC road and pavers laid in the buffer space between the CC 
road and the sidewalk in the middle will be damaged. 
The restoration of White thin topping and reconstruction of CC pavers along with foot path pavers is 
included in the estimate. Apart from this reconstruction of road on approaches Hosur Road near St. Jhon 
property and on Sarjapura Road in the premises Survey of India office. Further the entire flexible area 
of the pavement along the corridor from Old Madras Road to Begur Road junction of 11.625Km is 
proposed for overlay of 40mm Bituminous Concrete (BC) and 60mm of Dense Bituminous Concrete 
(DBM) used for profile correction to correct the undulations including desilting of the existing drain 
with repairs at damaged locations.
