4.4 Structural type Proposed for Elevated Corridor 
Various structural arrangement options were studied for the proposed flyover based on: 
 Functional requirement. 

=== PAGE 129 ===
 
123 
 
 Characteristics of subsoil. 
 Facilities to be provided at grade. 
 Ease in construction. 
 Economy etc. 
 Topography of Site condition. 
Accordingly, the structural system was planned taking into account suitability of the same at the proposed 
location, constructability, level of impact on traffic movement during construction etc. Elevated Corridor is 
planned based on subsoil report.  
4.4.1 Superstructure of Elevated Corridor 
The type of material proposed for the superstructure is UHPFRC PSC open U box girder.  
The relative thicknesses of the web, top, and bottom flanges are determined through a design process that 
considers factors like span, loading, and relevant design codes, as outlined by the code of applicable countries 
and Indian Road Congress (IRC).  
An open box U girder as a supporting structure for flyover is primarily proposed to efficiently carry loads over 
long spans, especially in horizontal curves or situations where a shallow section is needed. The main purpose 
is to maximize structural efficiency by minimizing weight while maximizing flexural stiffness and load-
carrying capacity, making it suitable for flyover applications.  
In this Segmental U box girders, the thickness of the web, and bottom flanges is not a fixed ratio but rather a 
design decision based on the specific bridge's span, loading conditions, and other factors.  
Web Thickness: The web thickness of the box girder is provided with 200mm for span length of 45m.  
Bottom Flange Thickness: Minimum thickness: 1/20th of the clear web spacing at the junction with the 
bottom flange, or 200mm, whichever is more. Purpose is to resist tensile forces and provide additional 
torsional stiffness. Proposed bottom flange is designed for 250mm. 
4.4.2 Substructure 
4.4.2.1 Pier Cap 
Cast-insitu/Precast Pier Cap is designed to be fixed as an integral part with the pier with solid concrete unit. 
The pier cap length is designed to accommodate the box girders. The length of pier cap is 6m for 17.2m 
Carriage way which can accommodate the Sturted box girders. 
The height of the pier with bottom of pier cap is based on the clearance requirement. Where vehicles have to 
cross below, minimum required vertical clearance of 5.5 m is ensured. At other support locations, the pier 
height varies according to the road profile. 
4.4.2.2 Pier 
The proposed substructure is a circular pier. The circular pier provides good structural integrity and resistance 
to impact, while the pier cap distributes loads from the bridge deck or superstructure to the pier, ensuring a 
stable and even distribution of forces.  

=== PAGE 130 ===
 
124 
 
Circular piers are often chosen for their strength and ability to resist both axial loads (from the bridge 
deck) and lateral loads (from wind, seismic activity, or vehicle impacts). Their rounded shape allows for 
a more even distribution of stress compared to rectangular or square piers, especially under off-center 
impacts. A single pier is proposed for the four lane superstructure. A circular pier with 2m is proposed. 
4.4.3 Foundation 
The foundation type depends on the subsoil nature and condition at the location. Bore holes taken at site 
were studied to get a fair idea of the subsoil composition and to decide on the suitable founding levels. 
Various tests conducted at the field and laboratories were reviewed to assess the subsoil composition and 
nature. The Soil strata at the location of construction of Elevated Corridor is very hard and stiff with Non- 
cohesive soil. As could be seen from the borehole drawings based on geotechnical investigation report, 
the hard strata could be seen below the ground at a depth of 25-30 m with N- value greater than 100.  
Based on the test results, it is proposed that the foundation of the structure has to be taken deep with the 
purpose of attaining a bearing strata which is suitable and which ensures strictly and durability of structure. 
Pile foundation of 1.2m dia is proposed with a pile group of 6Nos, the spacing and width of the pile cap 
is proposed as per IRC standards. 
4.4.4 Elevated Rotary Structure 
Madiwala Junction a T junction at Chainage: 8+782 with Underpass which is on Hosur Main Road is 
connecting mainly from Sarjapura/Ejipura Road with Hosur Road from St. John junction to Silk Board 
Junction after studying various structural options for designing this junction with all possible views to 
meet this junction and IRC standards. The following options of structures were studied. The type of 
structure is chosen based on their advantages and disadvantages. 
a. Elevated Rotary 
b. Clover leaf 
c. Underpass 
Elevated Rotary: The elevated rotary concept was chosen based on the advantages of its circular type, 
which enables access from all directions without traffic conflict. Other advantages are that it can be 
accommodated on available land. High speed operations of heavy traffic movement at this junction can 
be handled with low speed due to short weave distance. This type of structure is a significant advantage 
as there is slow traffic. 
 
Clover Leaf: Options for the Clover Leaf Interchange were tried by designing one loop ramp for right-
turning traffic and one outer connection for left-turning traffic in each quadrant. These types of structures 
require more than one structure of different levels and providing approaches is a big problem. The land 
acquisition area required to be more for providing service roads along the approaches. 

=== PAGE 131 ===
 
125 
 
At the planning stage it was found that this type of structure would not suit the proposed and existing 
railway lines. Also turns out to be very expensive. 
Underpass: The scheme of underpass at this junction is inconvenient since already and underpass is 
constructed at the center of road and extending underpass further was not feasible and completely blocked 
due to direct access without provision of left or right turns. Proper drainage system is also becoming a 
problem. 
After analyzing the above options in consultation with department, it was finally decided to plan to provide 
an elevated rotary concept to ease the traffic to overcome the land issue constraint and meet the 
requirements. 
Rotary Concept 
The concept of selecting the junction type at this location is as follows 
1. Vehicles entering the rotary are forced to slow down and continue moving slower speeds prevent 
vehicles from stopping at intersections. 
2. Traffic flow is regulated in one direction of movement only, thus eliminating severe collisions between 
crossing motions. 
3. This is ideally suited for medium or high traffic or at intersections with at least three or four 
approaches. 
4. Rotaries are self-governing and practically require no control by police or traffic signals. 
5. Due to reduction in speed of vehicles, severe conflicts and accidents are avoided and their intensity is 
less in rotaries. 
4.4.5 Geometric and Structural Design Standards 
The geometry of Madiwala junction includes open land in the middle having a circular shape and road 
development on all three sides, north-south and east-west. At present, this junction has a main entrance to 
connect the north-south dual carriageway.  
As per the site situation and ground reality, an attempt has been made to design the junction in an 
innovative way by raising a circular structure in the Centre in a rotary form to utilize the available land. 
Allow access to connect the road in all directions conflict-free by allowing the junctions to accommodate 
additional lanes required as they are planned for future lanes. To accommodate the heavy traffic of around 
2250veh/hr and 2510veh/hr, there is a need to channelize the traffic in the limited land with innovative 
design of elevated rotary as grade separator. 
Traffic rotary or roundabout is a special form of at-grade intersection in which one-way traffic flows 
around a large center island before weaving into the respective directions from the traffic flow emanating 
from the center island. In our country, the 'keep left' regulation is followed and the flow around the central 
island is clockwise. 

=== PAGE 132 ===
 
126 
 
In a rotary intersection there is a smooth and efficient flow of traffic. All traffic proceeds at a fairly uniform 
speed. Frequent stopping and starting of vehicles are avoided. Crossing movements are converted into 
weaving or merging and diverging operations. Direct conflict is eliminated. Thus the journey is more 
consistent and comfortable. 
The design of rotary elements requires special considerations depending on each site requirement. No 
standard design can be adapted to any particular site conditions. So it became necessary to design the 
intersection and increase the capacity of the road smooth and safe traffic movement. 
As this project road falls within urban limits, relevant IRC design standards are due consideration to the 
Latest directives and guidelines of MORT&H/IRC have been followed as far as possible while 
formulating design criteria. Other national and international standards are also cited where found relevant. 
The criteria for various components are explained below. 
4.4.6 Geometric Standards 
IRC: 86 – 1983, “Geometric Design Standards for Urban Roads in Plains”. 
IRC: 92-1985, “Guidelines for the design of interchanges in Urban areas” 
4.4.7 Design Speed 
The proposal at the junction is an elevated rotary. Therefore, the design speed of the rotary is less than 
that of the roads leading to it. Although it is possible to design a Rotary without reducing high speed, the 
geometry can cause large costs for large-scale construction. The normal design speed considered as 30 to 
35 kmph for urban areas. 
4.4.8 Carriageway Width.  
As per The traffic demand on the project road a 4-lane flyover is justified from the point of view of traffic 
capacity. 
Up/down ramps shall be established to ensure smooth access which induces efficient circulation of 
expected traffic in all directions. 
The maximum grade of approach road is set below max 5% to ensure climbing performance on slippery 
surfaces and to reduce accidents on steep slopes in winter and reduce vehicle traction, as evidenced by the 
results of site surveys on several road sections along steep gradient. 
 Based on the traffic demand as per the projections, four lane configurations are proposed for rotary 
flyover. An intermediate carriageway of minimum width is proposed for service roads with footpath cum 
drain of 2 m. 
4.4.9 Camber. 
A camber of 2.5% is proposed for rotary flyover and carriageway of service roads. 

=== PAGE 133 ===
 
127 
 
4.4.10 Super Elevation.  
Super elevation of rotary roads is ignored. But, here if the vehicle changes its direction to its opposite side 
it travels around the Central Island and changes direction. While shifting, the vehicle may turn or slip, to 
overcome this, a minimum lateral slope is provided which is nothing but camber. In rotary roads this 
camber acts as super elevation. 
4.4.11 Horizontal Geometry.  
A design speed of 35 kmph is proposed at the rotary flyover and Graded roads. Rotary design is based on 
the traffic operations of diverging, merging and weaving. The design elements include design speed, 
radius at entry, exit and the central island, weaving length and width, entry and exit widths are designed 
as per IRC-65. The Radius of Rotary is designed with minimum radius of 1.3 times of entry curve. The 
entry to the rotary is not in straight, but a small curvature of about 20 and 25 meters is introduced. The 
exit radius provided is 1.5 to 2 times than the entry radius of the rotary so that the vehicles will discharge 
from the rotary at a higher rate. The width of the weaving section is kept higher than the width at entry 
and exit preferably between 6 to 18m and weaving length available at the intersection is between 18 to 
25m.  The traffic rotary is designed as per standards and is reducing the complexity of crossing traffic by 
forcing them into weaving operations. 
 
4.4.12 Elevated Rotary and approaches. 
Considering the high traffic volume and complex movement patterns at this T-junction, an elevated rotary 
with a four-lane unidirectional flyover is proposed to reduce congestion. The junction lies in close 
proximity to both St. John’s Junction and Silk Board Junction, making it a critical node in the network. 
1. Design Concept 
a. 
The rotary flyover is designed to allow vehicles to move continuously without stopping. 
b. While vehicles do not halt, they may change direction or cross paths at reduced speeds. 
c. 
Standard city road speeds are not applicable at rotary intersections; traffic must pass at low, controlled 
speeds to ensure safety and minimize collision risks. 
d. Speed control panels will be installed at approaches to regulate movement through the rotary. 
2. Structural Features 
a. The rotary flyover will consist of fully curved sections along all approach arms, with only short 
straight lengths near the ends of the approaches. 
b. Soil slopes with earth-retaining structures are proposed beyond the abutments on all approach sides 
to ensure stability. 
c. A minimum vertical clearance of 5.50 m from the existing road level will be maintained at obligatory 
span locations, ensuring adequate passage for vehicles and compliance with standards. 

=== PAGE 134 ===
 
128 
 
Improvement proposals of Rotary 
Elevated Rotary and its geometric standards, lane width and median are designed as per IRC norms, 
Rotary Carriageway is four lane width of 14.0m each lane 3.5m wide with 0.5m RCC crash barriers 
provided on edge of rotary the total width of Rotary is 15.0m. The main features are shown below. 
Features of Elevated Rotary at Madiwala T Junction 
Proposed Location             : Madiwala T Junction at Chainage: 8+782 
Number of Lane              : 4 lanes unidirectional 
Total width of Elevated Rotary      : 15.0m 
Minimum Carriageway Width      : 4 x 3.5 m 
Crash Barrier                : 2 x 0.5m 
Minimum Vertical Clearance       : 5.50m 
Radius of the Rotary            : 36.0m 
Overall Radius of the Rotary       : 51.0m

Approaches 
All approaches to the Elevated Rotary Flyover at Madiwala T junction is already explained above under 
details of proposal for the proposed elevated corridor from Sl. NO. 4 to 14 
4.4.13 Utility Relocation Plan 
Proposed Project corridor is falling within the thickly populated and developed area hence all types of 
utilities are existing in the corridor such as Water Supply ad sewage lines (BWSSB), High Tension Power 
Line of 11 KV (BESCOM/KPTCL), GAS Pipeline/OFC. After consultation with concerned stake holders 
a lump sum provision of 8 % on the cost of Amount put to tender is reserved for shifting/reconstruction 
of Utilities falling within the project corridor. 
There are 89 trees falling along the proposed flyover alignment, out of 37 trees are proposed for 
transplantation and 52 trees is have to be felled during the construction phase. As a compensatory measure, 
it is proposed to plant the number of trees as per forest department direction to be felled with site specific 
indigenous species. Apart from this there are 70 trees found to be partially affecting the alignment which 
are proposed for the cutting of branches only. The cost for the same is included in the estimate.

5.1.6 Traffic Capacity of Project Road 
The peak hour traffic / hour as estimated at this project road for the present year of count is 3996 to next 
25 years will be12064.  
As per IRC-106 1990 “Guidelines for Capacity of Urban Roads in Plain Areas” the recommended design 
service volumes PCU/Hour for 6 lane divided two-way traffic is  4300 for sub-arterial road (with frontage 
access but no standing vehicles and high capacity intersections), and whereas for  4 lane divided two-way 
traffic is 2900.  
As per IRC guide lines/recommendations, carriageways at grade level should be designed for more than 
6lanes with divider for two way traffic to accommodate 5664 V/H traffic in next 15 years in the project 
road. This leads to be land acquisition problem throughout the length of project and acquiring the land in 
urban area like Bengaluru is very costly and entire length of project is built up area and at some length 
with defense land. 
As the PCU/hr at present year is 3996, at least 50% of the long run traffic will use elevated structure to 
reach their destination in short time by avoiding hurdles of junctions at grade road. Other benefits of 
constructing a high-rise structure on the project road include connecting different parts of the city and 
reducing traffic congestion. Several elevated corridors are planned to address the city's traffic problems, 
and this particular stretch is part of that plan 
Bengaluru is experiencing rapid urbanization and a corresponding increase in vehicle traffic, leading to 
severe congestion on major roads like Old Madras Road and the route through Indiranagara. Existing 

=== PAGE 141 ===
 
135 
 
roads are often narrow and heavily developed, making widening at the existing level difficult and leading 
to land acquisition issues.  
When a four-lane elevated structure is constructed, most of the long-haul traffic is diverted to the elevated 
structure, and this leads to the relief of traffic congestion on the grade road. There will be an 8-lane 
carriageway along the entire length, where an additional 4-lane elevated structure can be constructed on 
the existing 4-lane road without land acquisition except at certain locations, if required.  
After discussion with BSMILE officials the concept of a four lane divided elevated corridor with 
carriageway of total width 17.20m has been proposed with two lane of 3.50m along with paved shoulder 
of 1.50m width, 0.5m shy off, 0.50m  crash barrier  and 0.60m median on either sides. 
Therefore the project is justified for the proposal of improvement of project with Elevated corridor with 
four lane carriageway. 
 
5.1.7 Traffic Study for Toll Assessment 
A comprehensive traffic study has been conducted for the Indiranagara–Silk Board corridor, and the study 
analyzed vehicle counts, diversion patterns, and willingness‑to‑pay percentages across all categories of 
traffic. 
From this analysis, it is revealed that the tollable traffic volume on this corridor alone is not sufficient to 
justify toll collection. The corridor lies within a busy city area of Bengaluru, where traffic is highly mixed 
and fragmented between elevated structures, down ramps, at‑grade roads, and existing flyovers. While 
diversion rates and toll willingness were assessed, the resulting tollable traffic count remains modest and 
does not support the establishment of toll plazas exclusively for this stretch. 
In view of these findings, the toll traffic count has not been considered for toll operations. The project is 
therefore proposed to be implemented under the EPC Mode of contract, ensuring that the infrastructure is 
delivered efficiently and made available for public usage without the burden of toll collection. This 
approach balances financial responsibility with public convenience, allowing the corridor to serve as a 
free‑flowing urban mobility improvement for Bengaluru.

7 Project Cost 
7.1 Bill of Quantities 
Total item wise quantities for Elevated corridor proposal for the project road are calculated as per the 
detailed drawings. Separate heads for all different items of work is included in the BOQ. The separate 
heads considered are detailed below:  Civil Works, GST, Provisional cost, and Total project cost. 
7.1.1 Civil Works 
The project cost under civil works includes everything related to implementation of civil works including 
improvements of at grade roads, flyover/bridges, street lighting electrical works, and environmental works 
of plantation of trees, rain water harvesting works. Following are the subhead estimates under civil works. 
7.1.1.1 Road works 
Bill-1 Pre- construction Activities, Site Clearance & Dismantling 
The quantity has been worked out for, dismantling of existing median/ white toping /flexible pavement, 
tree cutting and side drains for foundation works. The dismantling works are worked out based on the 
Proposed Typical cross sections. 
Bill-2 Foundation (Pile &Pile cap): 
The quantification of the Bored cast-in-situ piles including pile cap etc., are based on the measurement as 
per IRC standards shown in the typical cross sections. All the items which are related to completion of 
foundation up to Pile cap is considered in the estimate. 
Bill No - 3 Sub Structures (Pier & PSC Pier cap) 
The quantities related to substructure items pier, PSC precast pier cap including Pedestals, bearing, 
diaphragm, HYSD steel requirement, etc., are measured based on the typical cross sections in linear 
measurements.  
Bill- 4 Super Structures (UHPFRC - U Girder) 
The quantities of UHPFRC U girder, I Girder using as beam for portal frame, deck slab, etc., calculation 
for sub base has been worked out based on detailed LB&D dimension as per drawings. 
Bill - 5 Reinforced Earth Approach ramps to super structures (RE Wall) 
The quantification of Fascia of RE Panel, earthwork, geo reinforcement, friction slab etc., are considered 
based on the typical cross sections. The quantity calculation for these items has been worked out based 
on detailed LB&D dimension as per drawings. 
 
 

=== PAGE 144 ===
 
138 
 
Bill- 6Wearing Coarse for super structures  
Stone Matrix Asphalt SMA with 3mm APP Polymer modified bituminous membrane is proposed the 
quantification of wearing course of Stone Matrix Asphalt is based on the typical cross sections showing 
the thickness and width of pavement layers. 
Bill-7Miscellaneous, finishing items for super structures (Crash barrier, painting, Handrail etc.) 
Quantity of Crash barrier, Drainage spout, down take pipe, external painting, Hand rail on crash barrier, 
pavement marking and road appurtenances etc., are estimated based on the standard design of IRC and 
quantification is based on road drawings and alignment plan. 
Bill No - 8 Streetlight over flyover 
The detailed quantity estimates for different components of street light over elevated corridor and its 
approaches has been worked out based on detailed LB&D dimension as per drawings. 
Bill No -9 Noise Barrier for controlling noise on flyover 
A separate quantity estimate has been worked out under this bill for Noise barrier components. The 
estimate is based on location of sound pollution. 
Bill No - 10 Restoration of existing road after completion of flyover construction, 
The quantity for restoration of road into original condition is assessed and is included in the estimate. The 
restoration items like, resurfacing of flexible pavement with sub base to wearing coat, for thin white 
topping dry lean concrete with Thin white topping items are included. Apart from the above, delisting 
relaying of kerb, footpath tiles etc., are also considered in the estimate. 
Bill No - 11Rainwater Harvesting 
Estimate also includes the provision for construction of Rain water harvesting of 4 in numbers at the 
locations as identified by the client. This includes brick work, aggregates size ranging from 1.5mm to 
boulder type as per IRC Standards.  
Bill – 12Design charges @ 0.5% on total of above items 2 to 9 as per SR V-1 Sl. No 1 Page 117 as it 
is EPC contract. 
Estimate also have provision of design charges as this contract is going to be call under EPC/HAM 
contract 
Bill – 13 Maintenance charges 
As per MORT&H standard, for stand-alone projects like Bridge/ Tunnel /flyover works: maintenance 
charges are calculated. The contractor shall be paid @ 0.25% of the Contract Price each for the first five 
years and @ 0.50% of the Contract Price each for the remaining period of five years. on total of items as 
per MORTH Ltr No.RW/NH-37010/4/2010-EAP(Printing) Vol.-IV Government of India Ministry of 
Road Transport & Highways Dated: 05.03.2019 AT Model agreement page 76 under section 14.1

7.2 Rate Analysis 
The unit rates have been arrived by considering the basic rates, lead distances, man power, machinery, 
and materials. The unit rate for every individual item will be arrived based on State PWD&IWTD 
Schedule of Rates, Bangalore Circle, for the year 2024-2025. Issue rates published by PWD were 
considered at the time of preparation estimate, i.e. 08-10-2025 & 06-01-2026. 
An additional percentage for shuttering works as specified in the SR has been considered in the unit rate 
of the particular concrete items, according to which the total unit rates have been assessed. 
Project area of construction of Elevated corridor shall be executed in the GBA limits, according to PWD 
SR 2024-25; the weightages of 10 percentages is added for finished items of BOQ in rate analysis.   
PWD SR for Electrical works 2024-25 is used for estimating the Street light works. 

=== PAGE 146 ===
 
140 
 
BWSSB SR 2024-25 and Common Schedule of Rates is used. 
Indian Railway Unified Standard Schedule of Rates 2019 is used to estimate the cost of transportation of 
UHPFRC Girders /Pre cast pier cap from casting yard to project site for innovative structure of elevated 
corridor. This SR is also used to derive the data rate. 
The Items which are not available in any of state SR, the rate analysis for such items are derived from the 
available SR items and are shown in rate analysis estimate sheets enclosed at the end of this chapter.

7.4 Compensatory afforestation 
According to the NGT and the Ministry of Environment, it is proposed to plant the trees at a ratio of 1:10, 
i.e. planting ten trees against losing one tree.   
In this project, about 235 trees are coming under construction width of corridor, most of the tress its 
branches are affecting, however it is planned to plant around 2350 fresh plantations wherever possible in 
and around Bengaluru city area. 
An amount of ₹4.26 crore has been provisioned towards arboriculture and afforestation under the 
estimated project cost. 
7.5 Re-usage of released materials 
Various items of materials dismantled from existing RCC flyover, drain, footpaths, curbs and covered 
drains, etc., are proposed for to maintain a proper account and are proposed for recycled or auctioned 
based on quality of the items observed. The amount of material. 
BSMILE have been requested to put a following specific condition in the Tender document for re-usage 
materials from dismantled items that,  
1. All salvage materials as listed in the following Schedule of Credit shall become the property of 
contractor (when the work is awarded on tender basis) and salvage is already affected as deduction in 
the total project cost. 
2. The materials enumerated below shall become the property of the contractor. These materials shall be 
allowed to be removed from site as the salvage value is already adjusted in the project cost. 
3. The rate fixed by department is firm. The contractor shall have no claim whatsoever against the 
department if actual rate turns out to be different from the rates inserted by the department. 
4. The amount of credit in respect of items shown below shall be provisional and actual quantities shall 
be measured in MB against each item shall be considered for arriving total amount of credit. 
5. Items if any obtained from demolition/dismantling but not listed below shall become the property of 
government and accordingly shall be handed over to BSMILE without any extra payment.

7.7 Justification for Project Cost 
NHAI data (Normative Cost) for cost-estimates of National Highways is updated by the Ministry of Road 
Transport and Highways from time to time and a standard cost per square meter for roads and structures 
has been arrived at based on various assumptions. 
The standard costs obtained on the basis of NHAI circular dated 25/04/2018 are being used only for 
comparison during appraisal and approval of projects. 
The standard cost of an elevated flyover with high strength concrete prepared by MORT&H is as follows 
and is compared with BSMILE elevated projects proposed for construction with UHPFRC superstructure 
elements. 
Although the table below indicates that the initial cost of structures using UHPFRC is higher than the 
standard construction cost, the following facts demonstrate that the use of UHPFRC remains economical 
and justified when compared to conventional methods.

The proposed elevated corridor is designed for execution using conventional method of PSC Box girder 
high-strength concrete of M-50 to M-60 grade. The estimated cost of structures has been worked out at 
₹1,18,405 per sq. m, reflecting an increase of more than 62% in cost per square meter when compared 
with conventional designs. 
However, by adopting Ultra-High Performance Fiber Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) for girders in the 
superstructure, significant savings can be achieved: 
•  Approximately 34% reduction in piles, 42% in piers, and pier caps, resulting in both cost and time 
savings. 
•  For a normative single-span structure constructed with UHPFRC, the cost per square meter is assessed 
at ₹25,000 per sq. m, which is substantially lower.

•  The Ministry of Road Transport & Highways (MoRTH) has considered normative rates per square meter 
for works in rural and district areas, which do not reflect the higher costs associated with urban cities like 
Bengaluru. 
•  When compared with similar projects of the MoRTH and NHAI, such as the Nagpur DPR, the proposed 
project cost is justifiable. Table below justifies the initial cost per sqm of UHPFRC

8 Environmental Impact Assessment Study 
8.1 Back Ground 
Bengaluru’s vehicle population has been growing day by day and the city roads are being added roughly 
5 lakh vehicles every year. By June 2025 total vehicle population in Bengaluru is reached about 1.2 Crore, 
the number of non-transport vehicles such as two-wheelers and cars in the city had reached 1.06 Crore. 
According to State Transport Department, two-wheelers are numbering over 81.9 lakh followed by cars 
numbering 23.59 lakh. Number of transport vehicles stands at over 10.89 lakh. These numbers exclude 
the vehicles that come in and go out of the city. This out growing numbers vehicles are choking the city 
roads with frequent traffic jams and have made traffic police helpless. 
Further, for various known reasons public is using the independent modes of transportation for commuting 
on existing limited road network adding to the congestion, accidents, noise and air pollution. As per the 
report on Comprehensive Traffic and Transportation Plan for Bangalore (CTTP) – 2011, an average 
Bangalore an spends more than 240 hours stuck in traffic every year and such delays are resulting in loss 
of productivity, in addition to deterioration of air quality, reduced quality of life, and increase in costs for 
services and goods. 
The concept of elevated corridor evolved to counter the traffic woes of the city in the form of traffic 
congestion and provide solution for many of the traffic related problems. The project is envisaged to 
separate the cross-city traffic and the neighborhood traffic, with an aim to decongest the core city and 
provide easy access to suburban towns and Bengaluru International Airport. The project is planned based 
on two traffic directions of the city - North-South and East-West, with interconnecting and loop elevated 
corridors. The basic idea is to decongest at grade roads by creating an additional capacity in the form of 
elevated corridors. This enhances the traffic carrying capacity of the roads at grade. As the widening of 
Bengaluru city roads is not possible, we are only left with the option of going vertical by elevated 
corridors. 
In this context, Government of Karnataka has planned for elevated corridors to ensure safe, fast and 
congestion free connectivity to different parts of city.  The issues relating to traffic and transportation in 
a large and growing city like Bangalore need to be viewed in the larger perspective of urban planning and 
development. Issues relating to land use planning and development control, public-private transportation 
policy and industrial location would need to be integrated at the perspective planning level. With Metro 
Rail under implementation there is the need to coordinate inter modal transport issues. 

=== PAGE 158 ===
 
152 
 
8.2 Land Use and Roadside Environments 
The observed land use pattern is predominantly residential layout formed by the Bangalore Development 
Authority, as the city grew, residential units along the main road were leased out to commercial activities 
and the road itself became predominantly commercial. 
There are bus stops with shelters along the corridors, skywalks/foot over bridges, gantry sign boards, street 
lights like high mast lamps, etc. are also in place. The project corridors are surrounded by important 
community structures such as schools, colleges and places of religious importance such as temples, 
mosques and churches. The implementation of the project will not affect these structures and the 
compound wall may need to be relocated in some places to avoid the impact. 
Some sections of the project corridors adjoining the land belong to Defense, public sectors like HAL and 
educational institutions. Some religious structures also exist 
The start of the project road, which deviates from the main road connecting the National Highway towards 
K R Puram and the heart of Bangalore city, is very close to the Eastern Terminal Railway Station and 
Metro Depot at Baiyappanahalli. This project road mainly connects K R Puram to Silk Board towards 
Hosur via Indiranagara and Domalur in the southeast of Bengaluru. The traffic intensity has increased due 
to the development of the areas around the project road. 
Traffic growth rates have been estimated based on the traffic flowing on the project road. The overall 
traffic growth rates for the next 25 years have been estimated considering the annual growth rate (%) of 
various vehicle types as mentioned in the above Table-6.1 and in Fig 6.1 in chapter 6-  
As per the traffic study the project corridor carries a traffic of 3996 vehicles/hour at peak time, whereas 
the maximum capacity of traffic that can be handle by project road at present for a road width of 20m with 
four lane is about 2800 vehicles/hour. There is now a need to widen the road to accommodate the next 5 
to 25 years traffic growth of 4833 to 12064 vehicles per hour. 
Since widening of the roads in Bengaluru city is not possible, we are left with the only option of going 
vertically through elevated corridors. 
This elevated corridor aims to separate inter-city traffic and neighborhood traffic, reducing congestion in 
the major city and providing easy access to suburban towns and the Bangalore International Airport. The 
basic idea is to reduce congestion on at-grade roads by creating additional capacity in the form of elevated 
corridors. This will increase the traffic capacity of at-grade roads. 
8.2.1 Climate 
Bengaluru has a tropical Savanna climate with distinct wet and dry seasons. January is the coolest month 
in Bangalore, with an average low temp of 15.1°C. April is the hottest, with an average high of 34.1°C. 
The highest temperature ever recorded in the city was 39.2°C on April 24, 2016.  The lowest temperature 

=== PAGE 159 ===
 
153 
 
ever recorded was 7.8°C in January 1884. In winter, usually the temp doesn't fall below 14°C, and in 
summer go above 36°C. The city gets rain from both the north east and southwest monsoons. The city 
gets a yearly rainfall of around 975 mm on average, with significant variations throughout the year. 
September typically receives the highest rainfall, followed by October and August. The period from June 
to October generally accounts for the majority of the annual rainfall. The graphical representation is shown 
in the figure below.  The average speed of the wind is 3.4 m/s, with a maximum of around 11 m/s. 
 
8.2.2 Humidity 
The average annual relative humidity in Bengaluru is 65.2% and average monthly relative humidity ranges 
from 45% in March to 79% in August. On an average, July is the most humid and January is the least 
humid month. 
8.2.3 Temperature 
The highest temperature ever recorded in Bengaluru was 41.8°C in Kengeri on April 30, 2024. The lowest 
temperature recorded was 7.8°C in January 1884.

9 Review of Detailed Project Report by Expert Committees 
The Detailed Project Report (DPR) concludes with an overall summary of the project, along with 
recommendations and compliances relevant to its implementation.  
9.1 Expert Committees Review  
The DPR was reviewed by the following Technical committees, and their discussions, observations, and 
compliance notes are also detailed below: 
1. GBA Technical Advisory Committee – 22.08.2025 
2. Bengaluru Metro Rail Corporation Limited (BMRCL) – 13.11.2025 
3. Directorate of Urban Land Transport (DULT) – 29.01.2026 to 06.02.2026 
4. Traffic Police Commissioner Bengaluru 
5. Bengaluru Electricity Supply Company (BESCOM) 
6. Bengaluru Water Supply & Sewerage Board (BWSS&B) 
7. State Level Environment Impact Assessment Authority- Karnataka 
8. National Highways Authority of India Regional Office Bengaluru  
9.1.1  Discussion at GBA Technical Advisory Committee Meeting 
During the preliminary stage of the project, prior to review by the Technical Committee, the Detailed 
Project Report (DPR) was prepared with a proposal for a four-lane elevated flyover incorporating an 
elevated rotary interchange at the Swami Vivekananda Metro Station. 
The elevated four-lane structure along 80 Feet Road from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station in 
Indiranagar was designed to continue and integrate with the existing Domlur flyover interchange. 
Following the Domlur interchange ramp towards Koramangala, a new proposal was introduced for an 
elevated four-lane flyover extending into the built-up area, with a gap at the existing approach ramp. The 
proposal for a flyover from Defence Land to Ejipura Junction was not recommended. 
The elevated flyover currently under construction up to St. John’s Hospital in Ejipura has been retained 
without modification. It is further proposed to continue the elevated road through the Madiwala underpass, 
connecting from the Ejipura approach to the Madiwala side, and extending through Silk Board Junction 
to link with the existing Electronic City Flyover. 
The DPR, detailing the alignment of the elevated corridor from Swami Vivekananda Metro Station to Silk 
Board Junction, was presented at the Technical Committee meeting. The estimated project cost for the 
proposal was ₹1,202 crore. The DPR was reviewed by committee experts during the meeting held on 22-
08-2025. 
After reviewing the DPR and conceptual plan, the committee members suggested corrections and 
modifications to ensure seamless integration of the proposed project with Silk Board Junction and the 

=== PAGE 164 ===
 
158 
 
Electronic City Flyover, including the provision of loops connecting to the Double-Decker Flyover on 
both sides. 
Modification of DPR 
Following the committee’s recommendations, the DPR was revised in consultation with officials of the 
BSMILE office. Key modifications included: 
The Detailed Project Report (DPR) has been modified to propose an elevated two-lane flyover from 
Indiranagar, eliminating the earlier plan for a flyover beyond the Domlur interchange approach on the 
Koramangala side. 
At the Madiwala underpass, the concept plan has been revised to integrate: 
a) The under-construction Ejipura flyover, 
b) The proposed flyover from Adugodi to Madiwala underpass, and 
c) The alignment with the existing double-decker metro flyover from Ragigudda and the Hosur Road 
flyover at Silk Board Junction, ensuring seamless connectivity to the Electronic City Flyover. 
The project cost has accordingly been revised to ₹1,300 crore. 
9.1.2 Review by various Organisations of Government  
 
The Director (Technical), BSMILE, has formally communicated through official letters to various 
Government organisations, as listed above section 9.1, to inform them about the major project of a 126.31 
km Elevated Corridor currently under planning and approval for implementation. Copies of these letters 
addressed to the respective departments are annexed in this project report. 
In addition, the technical heads of these organisations were invited to the BSMILE office, where a detailed 
presentation on the proposed 126 km Elevated Corridor in Bengaluru was delivered. The purpose of this 
presentation was to alert the departments and ensure coordination in project implementation, particularly 
to integrate any ongoing or proposed schemes that may overlap with the corridor alignment. This proactive 
step was taken to avoid complications and delays in resolving conflicts at a later stage. 
All departmental officers attended the presentation, sought clarifications, and assured BSMILE that any 
major planning initiatives within their respective departments would be discussed and clarified in advance. 
Public service agencies such as BESCOM and BWSSB were specifically alerted regarding their utility 
services existing along the proposed corridor routes. 
Furthermore, the BSMILE office has sought information from the Karnataka State Pollution Control 
Board (KSPCB) regarding clarifications on environmental clearance required for the implementation of 
the Elevated Corridor project.

The Project Director of the NHAI Bengaluru Regional Office also presented and reviewed matters related 
to the integration of flyovers with the NHAI Corridor, ensuring coordination between the two

The Director (Technical), BSMILE, requested the Managing Director of BMRCL to review the DPR

suggestions through office letter No. B-Smile/ni (tham)/PR/329/2025-26 dated 13/10/2025.

office, 2nd Floor, BMTC B-Block, K.H. Road, at Shanthinagara Bengaluru. The meeting was attended by 
the Director (Technical), Chief Engineer, and other officers of BSMILE along with the Consultants. The 
session was chaired by Shri Hegga Reddy, Chief Engineer, BMRCL, along with their Technical team. 
A presentation was made to BMRCL covering the project corridor alignment and key Technical aspects 
of planning and construction methodology. The alignment loop deviating from  Ragigudda double decker 
flyover and where the proposed flyover intersects or runs parallel to the Metro corridor from Hosur to Silk 
board junction, was reviewed in detail.  
BMRCL agreed in principle to the concept of the alignment passing through and running parallel to the 
Metro corridor, subject to confirmation of adequate vertical / horizontal clearance for the flyover. 
Overall, BMRCL conveyed their concurrence with the DPR, noting no major observations.

10 . Economic Internal Rate of Return. 
10.1 Introduction 
 
The objective of this cost-benefit economic analysis is to identify and quantify the associated benefits and 
costs of the proposed project. This analysis is designed to facilitate the identification of the optimal 
solution and assess its economic viability in terms of potential investment returns. The study is conducted 
to confirm the economic feasibility of the project road and to assist the Government in its decision-making 
process. The economic viability of implementing the Elevated Road project is thoroughly examined. 
Since projects of this nature necessitate the utilization of scarce resources and significant public 
investment thereby incurring social costs it is imperative to conduct a rigorous economic analysis to 
determine whether the resulting benefits to society and the economy are substantial enough to justify the 
investment. 
The economic feasibility study is executed in adherence to the overall guidelines stipulated by the Indian 
Road Congress (IRC: SP: 30-2009), the World Bank in their respective manuals, and the Manual for 
HDM-4. These methodologies and manuals are recognized and accepted by the Ministry of Road 
Transport & Highways (MORT&H), the National Highways Authority of India (NHAI), and the World 
Bank for application in highway projects across India. 
The Analysis of Cost vs. Benefit is for proposed construction of the new elevated corridor from –Old 
Madras Road from Swamy Vivekananda Metro Station to Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road via 
Indiranagara – Domlur - Madivala road and is anticipated to yield several key benefits, including a 
reduction in vehicle operating costs, travel time, accidents, and environmental pollution. In the present 
analysis, the cost stream is comprised of the civil construction costs, while benefits are primarily accrued 
from savings in vehicle operating costs (VOC) and reduced travel time costs for both passengers and 
goods. Secondary benefits, such as social development, environment and an improved quality of life, are 
also considered significant project benefits and have been quantified separately based on findings from 
earlier studies. 
The cost-benefit analysis is performed using the discounted cash flow (DCF) technique to derive the 
economic internal rate of return (EIRR) and the economic net present value (ENPV) for the proposed 
investments linked to the project. This analysis is followed by a comprehensive sensitivity analysis, which 
tests the impact of increasing or decreasing critical factors affecting the project's cost and benefit streams. 
The sensitivity analysis aims to ascertain the effects of these variables on the primary economic feasibility 
indicators (EIRR and ENPV). 
 

=== PAGE 172 ===
 
166 
 
10.2 Traffic Survey and Economic Evaluation 
As of 2025, Old Madras road to Silk board serves as one of Bengaluru’s most saturated arterial corridors, 
facilitating critical connectivity between Old Madras road and Hosur road. To evaluate the long-term 
feasibility of proposed infrastructure improvements, such as the planned elevated corridor or road 
widening, a comprehensive traffic survey was conducted to determine the Annual Average Daily Traffic 
(AADT) for the base year 2025. This study utilizes classified traffic volume counts to establish current 
density patterns and peak hour characteristics. By applying a compounded annual growth rate of 5%, 
traffic volumes are projected over a 25-year horizon to model future demand. These projections serve as 
the primary data input for calculating the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR), ensuring the project's 
viability against the opportunity cost of capital while addressing chronic congestion at key bottlenecks 
like the Silk Board junction.  
 
10.2.1  Projected traffic  
 
All modern road and urban infrastructure projects require fundamental traffic volume inputs to evaluate 
their performance over a defined lifespan. A 25-year projection allows engineers to assess the Level of 
Service (LOS) and identify potential bottlenecks well before they occur. Calculating Vehicle Operating 
Costs (VOC) over this period provides a data-driven baseline for comparing alternative road designs, 
such as widening existing lanes versus constructing new flyovers. This analysis utilizes to translate 
projected traffic flow and pavement roughness into tangible financial impacts for road users.  The 
projected traffic for this project is shown in below table below.

10.3 Financial Models Overview for the proposed elevated corridor 
 
The primary financial models for the proposed elevated corridors in Bengaluru involve Public-Private 
Partnerships (PPP), specifically an annuity model or hybrid annuity model (HAM), often coupled 
with toll collection.  
1. Annuity Model/Hybrid Annuity Model (HAM): This model has been a key consideration for 
ensuring financial viability. Under an annuity model, the government pays the private 
concessionaire a fixed, semi-annual annuity over a specific concession period (e.g., 25 years). 
This provides a steady return for the private developer. In a hybrid annuity model, the government 
also bears a portion of the initial project cost (around 40-50%), with the private entity raising the 
balance through loans. 
2. Tolling and Revenue: The financial models rely on collecting tolls from users to generate revenue 
and repay loans or provide returns to private partners. 
a) Implementing traditional toll plazas within the densely built metropolitan area is difficult due to 
lack of space. 
b) Alternative strategies such as Electronic Road Pricing (ERP) have been suggested for toll 
collection, though the feasibility of this in the local context has been a subject of debate. 

=== PAGE 174 ===
 
168 
 
 
c) Toll Collection Decision 
 
i. 
The proposal for toll collection along the Indiranagara–Silk Board corridor has been carefully 
evaluated. Due to the discontinuous nature of the alignment—comprising short elevated 
stretches interspersed with down ramps, at-grade roads, and existing flyovers—implementing 
toll plazas is not practical. 
ii. 
A fragmented elevated corridor would lead to operational challenges, user inconvenience, and 
inefficient traffic management. The anticipated traffic diversion and willingness-to-pay analysis 
further indicate that tolling would not generate sufficient value to justify the infrastructure and 
operational costs. 
iii. 
Accordingly, toll collection has been dropped for this project. The corridor will instead be 
executed under the EPC Mode of contract, ensuring smooth delivery, cost efficiency, and 
improved public acceptance without the complexities of toll operations. 
 
d) Infrastructure Cost Recovery through Property Tax 
 
Given that toll collection has been dropped for the Indiranagara–Silk Board corridor due to its 
discontinuous elevated alignment, an alternative mechanism for cost recovery is essential. One 
viable approach is to leverage property tax as a means of infrastructure funding once all planned 
elevated structures are completed and opened for public use. 
 
Rationale 
 
i. 
Public Accessibility: The corridor will be free of tolls, ensuring seamless usage for commuters 
and reducing congestion. 
ii. 
Equitable Contribution: Properties in the influence zone of the corridor—particularly those 
benefiting from improved connectivity, reduced travel time, and enhanced land value—can 
contribute proportionally through property tax. 
iii. 
Sustainable Financing: Linking infrastructure cost recovery to property tax ensures a steady 
revenue stream for the municipal body without burdening commuters directly. 
 
Implementation Approach 
 
iv. 
Integration with BBMP Property Tax System: The Bruhat Bengaluru Mahanagara Palike 
(BBMP) already collects property tax annually. A modest levy earmarked for infrastructure 
recovery can be incorporated into this existing framework. 
v. 
Value Capture Financing: Properties near the corridor are expected to appreciate in value 
due to improved accessibility. A small incremental tax can capture part of this value gain to 
fund the project. 
vi. 
Transparency and Accountability: Clear communication that the levy is dedicated to 
infrastructure repayment will build public trust and acceptance. 
Benefits 
vii. 
Avoids toll‑related operational challenges. 
viii. 
Ensures that those who benefit most from the project contribute fairly. 
ix. 
Provides long‑term financial sustainability for urban transport infrastructure in Bengaluru. 
x. 
Enhances public goodwill by keeping the corridor toll‑free while still recovering costs.                   

=== PAGE 175 ===
 
169 
 
3. Government Funding/Support: The state government has committed to partly funding the 
projects, including bearing the significant cost of land acquisition. Initial estimates for the 126 
km network were around ₹18,000 crore, with additional costs for land acquisition. 
 
4. Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV): A Special Purpose Company or Vehicle is often recommended 
to facilitate private sector participation and manage the project's execution and financing.  
10.4 Assumptions for study 
The analysis for the project road is being carried out considering the following assumptions; 
1. Analysis Period: A 25-year period (2027–2053) is considered as standard for high-value 
infrastructure. 
2. Speed Limits: Elevated corridors are designed to bypass congestion. Increase the speed limit 
from 30–40 km/hr to 60–80 km/hr to reflect the "Time Savings" benefit, which is the primary 
driver of EIRR. 
3. Pavement Condition (IRI): A new elevated structure typically features a smoother surface. 
Reduce the Roughness (IRI) from 2.0−2.5. 
4. Maintenance Costs: Elevated corridors involve lower routine pavement maintenance but 
significantly higher periodic structural maintenance (expansion joints, bearings, and drainage 
cleaning) compared to at-grade roads. 
5. Economic Conversion Factor: While 0.9 is a standard Shadow Price Rate, ensure it accounts 
for current GST structures and labour laws in 2025.  
10.5 Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR)  
 
The Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) is a metric used to determine if a public or development 
project is worth the investment from the perspective of an entire society or national economy. Following 
paras explain in detail on EIRR, covering Perspective of Analysis, Shadow Pricing & Adjustments, 
and Decision Criterion 
1. Perspective of Analysis: Unlike the standard Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) which 
only tracks monetary profits for a private investor, EIRR evaluates the "societal profitability" of 
a project. It captures broader impacts like time saved by commuters, job creation, and 
environmental improvements. 
 
2. Shadow Pricing & Adjustments: To reflect true economic value, EIRR uses "shadow 
prices" instead of market prices. This involves removing distortions like taxes and subsidies—
which are seen as internal transfers rather than resource costs—and accounting 
for externalities that market prices often ignore. 
 
3. Decision Criterion: A project is generally considered economically viable if its EIRR is greater 
than or equal to the Social Discount Rate (SDR) or the social opportunity cost of capital. 
 
 Common Benchmarks: Many international development banks, like the Asian Development 
Bank (ADB), traditionally used a benchmark of 12%. 
 
 Viability: If the EIRR exceeds this threshold, the project generates enough societal value to be 
worth the investment. 
 

=== PAGE 176 ===
 
170 
 
 
a. Alternatives assumptions 
 
To correct the project alternatives for an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) analysis of an 
elevated corridor, it should refine the descriptions to clearly distinguish between "at-grade" and "elevated" 
operations, as this is the primary driver of economic benefits like time savings and reduced vehicle 
operating costs.  
i.  Alternatives for EIRR Analysis 
 
Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) is a widely used metric for evaluating the economic viability 
of public projects. However, it has certain limitations, such as the potential for multiple rates of return 
with unconventional cash flows or conflicts with other methods when comparing mutually exclusive 
projects.  
Alternatives to EIRR analysis include other discounted cash flow (DCF) methods and various evaluation 
metrics:  
Net Present Value (NPV): This is the most widely recommended alternative to the IRR method. It 
calculates the absolute monetary value a project adds by discounting all future cash flows at a 
predetermined social discount rate or opportunity cost of capital. The project with the highest positive 
NPV is generally considered the best option. 
Benefit-Cost (B/C) Ratio or Present Value Ratio (PVR): This ratio compares the present value of total 
benefits to the present value of total costs. A ratio greater than one suggests the project is economically 
feasible.  
ii.  Alternative 1: With Project (Elevated Corridor Scenario) 
  
The "With Project" scenario involves evaluating the proposed investment, in this case, the construction of 
an elevated corridor. This analysis considers all the anticipated economic costs and benefits that would 
arise if the project were implemented.  
Key elements of this scenario's analysis include: 
Costs: Initial capital expenditure (civil costs, land acquisition), ongoing operation and maintenance costs, 
and any potential negative externalities (e.g., temporary construction disruption). 
Benefits: Quantifiable economic benefits such as reduced travel time, decreased vehicle operating costs, 
fewer accidents, reduced emissions (if applicable), and increased economic activity in the affected region. 
Assessment: The cash flows (economic costs and benefits) from this scenario are discounted to calculate 
an EIRR or NPV, which is then compared against a benchmark (e.g., the opportunity cost of capital) to 
determine its standalone viability.  
 

=== PAGE 177 ===
 
171 
 
iii.Alternative 2: Without Project (Do-Nothing Scenario)  
 
The "Without Project" scenario, often called the "Do-Nothing" or "Business as Usual (BAU)" alternative, 
serves as the baseline or control case for comparison. It evaluates the conditions and performance of the 
corridor if the proposed elevated road were not built.  
Key aspects of this baseline analysis: 
Baseline Conditions: The analysis establishes the existing conditions of traffic congestion, travel times, 
safety records, and environmental factors, projecting how these would evolve over the project's life 
without the new investment. 
Foregone Benefits: It calculates the costs incurred (or benefits foregone) by not acting. For example, it 
would account for the continued economic losses from severe traffic congestion, ongoing maintenance 
costs of the existing at-grade road, and potential increases in accidents over time. 
Comparative Basis: The true economic viability of the "With Project" alternative is determined by 
comparing its outcomes and net benefits against this "Without Project" baseline. The incremental benefits 
of the elevated corridor must exceed the incremental costs compared to the do-nothing scenario to be 
considered economically sound. 
b. EIRR Components to Include 
 
1. Capital Costs: Land acquisition (if any), civil construction of the elevated structure, and utility 
shifting. 
 
2. Maintenance Costs: Life-cycle maintenance for both the new elevated structure and the existing 
at-grade road. 
 
10.6 Economic Benefits: 
 
a) Travel Time Savings: This is a major benefit, resulting from increased travel speeds and 
reduced congestion on both the new elevated structure and the improved at-grade road. Time 
saved by both commercial vehicle operators (leading to increased productivity) and passengers is 
quantified and valued (often based on wage levels or a proportion of per capita GDP). 
 
b) Vehicle Operating Cost (VOC) Savings: Improved road conditions (smoother surfaces, better 
geometry) and smoother traffic flow lead to reductions in fuel and oil consumption, as well as 
less wear and tear on tires and other vehicle maintenance costs. 
 
c) Reduction in Accidents: Enhanced safety features and reduced conflict points between elevated 
and at-grade traffic lead to a decrease in accident rates. The economic value assigned includes 
the costs of property damage, injuries, fatalities, and associated legal or investigation expenses. 
 
d) Environmental Benefits (Reduction in Emissions): Less traffic congestion and smoother 
speeds can result in reduced idling fuel consumption and lower harmful vehicle emissions (e.g., 
CO2, pollutants), which can be quantified and valued as a social benefit. 
 

=== PAGE 178 ===
 
172 
 
e) Savings in Infrastructure Maintenance Costs: While maintenance is a cost, the project might 
lead to savings in maintenance costs for other parts of the network, or a more efficient overall 
maintenance regime, which would be counted as a benefit. 
 
f) Generated Traffic Benefits: The project might induce new traffic or economic activity that 
wouldn't have occurred otherwise (e.g., access to new markets, job creation). This increased 
economic activity can be estimated and included as a benefit. 
 
g) Residual Value of Assets: The estimated value of the infrastructure at the end of the analysis 
period is considered a benefit in the final year of the EIRR calculation. 
 
h) Broader Economic and Social Impacts: These can include non-quantifiable benefits that are 
sometimes estimated, such as: 
 
 Enhanced regional development, trade, and tourism. 
 Improved access to essential services like healthcare and education. 
 Increase in abutting land and property values.  
 
3.1 Sensitivity Analysis: Test the EIRR against a 10-15% increase in capital cost or a 10-20% 
reduction in traffic to ensure the project remains viable (EIRR > Social Discount Rate, typically 12% 
in India).  
c. Analysis of Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR)  
 
i. Alternative 1: With Project (Elevated Corridor Scenario)  
 
This Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) report evaluates the construction of a 13.049 km 
elevated corridor from Sir Vishvesvaraya Metro Station to Silk Board via Indiranagara-Domlur-
Madivala. As of December 2025, Bengaluru is aggressively expanding its road infrastructure with an 
allocation of ₹1.2 lakh crore for tunnel and double-decker roads to mitigate severe congestion.  
ii. Project Cost & Cash Outflows 
 
The total investment of ₹1300 Cr is distributed equally across two years (FY 2027-2028). 
Civil Cost: ₹852.00 Cr (65.53%). 
Other Costs: ₹448.00 Cr (34.47%), covering GST, land compensation, utility shifting, and DPR/PMC 
charges. 
O&M Costs: Estimated at ₹13.00 Cr per annum (1% of CAPEX), escalating at 5% annually.  
iii. Quantified Economic Benefits (Inflows) 
 
Benefits are calculated based on the 2025-2053 traffic projection starting with an AADT of 102,134c 
using standard Indian transport methodologies (IRC: SP: 30):  
1. Value of Travel Time (VOTT) Savings: Based on an average saving of ₹142/hr for cars and 
₹45/hr for two-wheeler, users in 2026 Bengaluru price, considering reductions in travel time 
(from ~90 mins to ~20 mins) for the AADT. 
 
2. Vehicle Operating Cost (VOC) Savings: Derived from smoother traffic flow and reduced fuel 
consumption during idling, and wear-and-tear due to consistent speeds (avg 40-50km/h vs

speeds. 
 
3. Pollution Reduction: Estimated 15–20% reduction in CO2 emissions from reduced idling at 
major chokepoints like Domlur and Madivala.  Lower idling at Silk Board and Indiranagar 
results in an estimated 2.87 kg CO2saving per trip for certain segments. 
 
4. Job Creation: Estimated multiplier of 200-250 man-years per ₹1 crore invested and estimated 
1,500+ direct construction jobs and long-term indirect employment from improved IT corridor 
connectivity.

d. Discussion on Environmental Factors 
The corridor effectively moves high-emission idling traffic to a steady-flow state. While construction has 
an initial carbon footprint (cement/steel), the net carbon balance becomes positive by Year 8 of operations 
due to massive savings in fuel consumption across the 102,134 daily vehicles.  
Conclusion 
The Proposed Elevated Corridor from Old Madras Road to Silk Board (Electronic City) is economically 
viable, with an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of 16.80%, which exceeds the benchmark hurdle 
rate of 12.00%. The Benefit Cost Ratio (BCR) of 1.99 indicates that for every ₹1.00 invested, nearly ₹2.00 
of economic benefits are realized. although the project cost is estimated to ₹1,300.00 Cr, substantial 
savings in Value of Time (VOT) and Vehicle Operating Costs (VOC) for over 100,000 daily commuters 
strongly justifies the investment. Strict adherence to the proposed 2 year implementation schedule is 
essential to avoid cost escalation. Additionally, the project is expected to stimulate growth in educational, 
healthcare, commercial, and industrial activities along the corridor. Considering the robust economic 
indicators along with the broader social and developmental benefits, the project is deemed economically 
viable and recommended for implementation.

11 Conclusion with Recommendation 
The adoption of precast pier caps and Ultra‑High Performance Fiber‑Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) 
girders marks a significant advancement in urban flyover construction. This approach is strongly 
recommended for its efficiency, durability, innovation, safety, and long‑term economic value, ensuring a 
robust, high‑quality, and cost‑effective infrastructure solution. 
Advantages: 
1.  Efficiency: Precast elements accelerate construction, reduce traffic disruption, and deliver predictable 
timelines. 
2.  Durability: UHPFRC offers exceptional strength, superior crack resistance, and extended service life 
with minimal maintenance. 
3.  Innovation: Factory‑controlled casting ensures precision and quality, while advanced materials enable 
longer spans and reduced foundation requirements. 
4.  Economic Viability: 
•  Although initial material costs are higher, comparative analysis demonstrates civil works savings of 
₹470 crore and an overall project cost reduction of ₹623 crore compared to conventional PSC girder 
technology. 
•  The incremental cost difference of ₹16,560 per sqm compared to standard rate is justified by reduced 
foundation works (~34%), faster execution timelines, and long‑term durability benefits. 
Construction Proposal: 
1. The project spans 11.625 km, from Swamy Vivekananda Metro Station to the Hosur Road – Electronic 
City Flyover, designed as a continuous elevated corridor. 
2. Integration points include: 
a. Domlur Interchange 
b. Existing Ejipura Flyover, ensuring uninterrupted at‑grade traffic flow. 
c. An elevated loop will connect traffic from Ejipura Flyover at St. John’s Hospital Junction to the 
Electronic City Flyover, with an interchange at Madiwala featuring an elevated rotary. 
d. The loop also integrates the proposed Audugodi–Electronic City Flyover. 
e. The total elevated length, including loops and approaches, is 13.049 km. 
3. Economic Assessment: 
i. The proposed Old Madras Road–Silk Board Elevated Corridor is economically viable, with: 
ii. EIRR: 16.8% (exceeding the 12.00% social discount rate) 
iii. BCR: 1.99, confirming that every ₹1.00 invested yields over ₹2.0 in economic returns.

iv. Benefits are primarily driven by commuter time savings and improved traffic efficiency.

efficiency:  
At ~₹65–₹80 Cr/km, Bengaluru’s proposed elevated corridors are well below national 
benchmarks (₹250–₹300 Cr/km for Mumbai/Chennai elevated corridors). 
Reason: 
 Use of UHPFRC girders (M-150–200) instead of conventional M50–60 concrete 
significantly reduces structural depth, material volume, and construction cycle time. 
Implication:  
Bengaluru corridors demonstrate how innovative materials and optimized widths can 
deliver elevated infrastructure at nearly 1/3rd the benchmark cost. 
 
Benchmark data from Mumbai, Delhi, Chennai, Nagpur, Pune, and Hyderabad demonstrates that 
conventional elevated corridors typically range between ₹240–₹305 Cr/km, with costs driven upward by 
complex urban interfaces, viaduct structures, and land constraints. In contrast, the proposed elevated 
corridors in Bengaluru, designed with Ultra High Performance Fibre Reinforced Concrete (UHPFRC) 

=== PAGE 184 ===
 
178 
 
girders of grade M-150–200 in place of conventional M50–60, achieve significantly lower costs of ~₹65–
₹80 Cr/km. This reduction is attributable to optimized corridor widths, reduced structural depth, lower 
material consumption, and faster construction cycles. The comparison substantiates that adoption of 
advanced materials and innovative design methodologies enables Bengaluru’s elevated corridors to be 
delivered at nearly one-third of the benchmark cost observed in other metropolitan regions, thereby 
ensuring both economic efficiency and technical robustness. 
Justification Note 
The comparative analysis clearly establishes that Bengaluru’s proposed elevated corridors, designed with 
UHPFRC girders, achieve civil costs of only ₹65 Cr/km, which is substantially lower than the national 
benchmarks of ₹240–₹305 Cr/km observed in Mumbai and Chennai. This cost efficiency is a direct result 
of adopting advanced materials, optimized corridor widths, and reduced construction cycle times. The 
data substantiates that UHPFRC technology delivers durable, high-performance structures at nearly one-
third the benchmark cost, without compromising safety or serviceability. 
Concluding Para 
The comparative assessment with benchmarks of Ministry of Road Transport and Highways and National 
Highways Authority of India indicates that the adopted cost parameters are within a reasonable range for 
urban elevated corridors, considering the specific project constraints. 
Although the initial cost associated with UHPFRC construction is higher than conventional benchmark 
values, the technology offers significant advantages including longer spans, reduced number of piers, 
lower interference with existing utilities, improved durability, and reduced maintenance requirements. 
These factors contribute to improved lifecycle performance and operational efficiency of the corridor. 
Further, the project has been conceptualized keeping in view the urban constraints, limited right-of-way, 
and the need to minimize disruption to existing traffic during construction. 
Based on the detailed analysis, the proposed project is found to be: 
 
Technically feasible  
 
Economically justified  
 
Environmentally beneficial in terms of congestion reduction  
In view of the demonstrated cost advantage and technical robustness of UHPFRC-based elevated 
corridors, it is recommended that the proposed Bengaluru corridors of Swami Vivekananda Metro Station 
to Silk Board Junction on Hosur Road be taken forward under the EPC mode of tendering. This approach 
will ensure timely execution, cost rationalization, and accountability, while aligning with national best 
practices and delivering significant savings compared to conventional elevated structures. 

=== PAGE 185 ===
 
179 
 
With an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of 16.80%, which exceeds the benchmark hurdle rate 
of 12.00%, along with associated social and developmental benefits, the project is economically viable 
and recommended for implementation. 
This project is proposed to execute under EPC (Engineering, Procurement and Construction) mode, 
wherein the contractor is paid by the client in stages as per the agreed payment schedule during 
construction and upon completion. Implementation under annuity or toll-based systems is challenging in 
dense urban areas, as toll collection is both impractical and socially sensitive. Instead, cost recovery for 
such infrastructure is more effectively achieved through appropriate urban taxation mechanisms across 
the city, enabling pooled funding for projects of this nature. 
For effective implementation, a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) under BSMILE has been established. The 
Board of Directors should be vested with full powers, and the Managing Director delegated adequate 
authority to ensure timely execution without bureaucratic delays.

=== PAGE 309 ===
 
303 
 
Advantages of UHPFRC Adoption 
•  Time Savings: Faster construction cycles reduce delays and associated cost overruns. 
•  Durability: UHPFRC ensures longer service life and reduced maintenance requirements. 
•  Efficiency: Optimized structural design reduces the need for multiple foundation elements. 
Incremental Cost Justification 
Several restoration and completion works are not included in the standard cost framework. Therefore, 
the incremental difference of ₹16,560 per sq. m between the normative cost and the tendered project 
cost (with UHPFRC structures) is also justified, given the urban context and the scale of construction.
